XII.5.2 Eastinthillation factory circuit
The Eastinthillation factory circuit, based on the Eastin Toffoli-state synthillation
protocol [24], is shown in
Fig. 18. The protocol uses eight |H⟩\ket{H}
states from the zero-level factories based on the C6 code to implement two
Margolus–Toffoli
gates with shared controls and distinct targets. Let pY,injp_{Y,\mathrm{inj}}
denote the stochastic logical-YY fault probability per |H⟩\ket{H}-state
injection used to implement a logical RY​(π/4)R_{Y}(\pi/4) rotation. A final
“acceptance” parity check on the targets
detects any single stochastic-YY input fault and yields a Toffoli state with
error probability of order pY,inj2p_{Y,\mathrm{inj}}^{2}.
We apply a Hadamard to the target qubit of the accepted Toffoli state. This
final Clifford converts it into a CCZ\mathrm{CCZ} resource state compatible with the
depth-one CCZ\mathrm{CCZ} gate of Sec. XII.4.
Figure 18: The Eastinthillation factory circuit.
We implement the Eastinthillation factory circuit in a single Q66 code block using the measurement-layer
sequence shown in Fig. 19. The
measurements use the LM1, LM2, and DM operations from the Walking Cat instruction
set [78], mediated by EDM (error-detecting measurement) and Viterbi measurement schemes.
We extend these schemes to include the Multi-Pauli error-corrected
measurement operation (MECM) introduced by Webster and Delfosse [81].
Every cat-state-mediated measurement layer in
Fig. 19 admits physical
representatives whose supports on the GB66 block are pairwise disjoint, with
each representative having weight at most 16. The complete representative
certificates are given in
appendix G. The relatively
small width of these representatives allows us to use only
3 rounds of EDM for each double-RY​(π/4)R_{Y}(\pi/4) injection to achieve
a pHp_{H} of order 10−810^{-8} at physical error rate p=10−4p=10^{-4}. Similarly,
we require only 5 rounds of Viterbi measurements to achieve logical error rates
of order 10−1310^{-13}. Each double-RY​(π/4)R_{Y}(\pi/4) injection ends in a
DMX – a destructive readout of all data qubits in the H6 block,
an operation which takes only 1 POC to execute regardless of width.
RY​(π/4)R_{Y}(\pi/4) injection.
Each pair of
RY​(π/4)R_{Y}(\pi/4) rotations is implemented with the double-RY​(π/4)R_{Y}(\pi/4)
injection gadget of the Walking Cat architecture [78], which involves a joint Y​YYY measurement
followed by a DMX operation on the C6 block.
To avoid correlated input errors, each Q66 block has two dedicated C6 source
blocks. A double-RY​(π/4)R_{Y}(\pi/4) layer takes one logical qubit, prepared in
|H⟩\ket{H}, from each C6 block rather than taking both inputs from one
encoded |H⟩⊗2\ket{H}^{\otimes 2} pair. The terminal DMX consumes both C6
blocks, including the unused logical qubit in each block. The two blocks are
then reinitialized and their next encoded |H⟩⊗2\ket{H}^{\otimes 2} pairs are
prepared and verified in parallel for the same Q66 block.
We use dedicated C6 blocks rather than sharing them among multiple Q66 blocks.
This choice simplifies the factory analysis by eliminating contention for shared resources.
Within either source C6 block, the two logical-YY operators have
representatives
Y¯0=YIYIYI,Y¯1=IYIYIY.\overline{Y}_{0}=\texttt{YIYIYI},\qquad\overline{Y}_{1}=\texttt{IYIYIY}.
(12)
These representatives have disjoint supports and weight three. Nevertheless,
either operator can be measured using a two-qubit cat-state segment (a Bell
state) by reusing one cat qubit for two data interactions. This reuse does not
reduce the measurement distance. A fault on the reused cat qubit can propagate
to two C6 data qubits. We choose the data-qubit support of each cat-state qubit
so that no resulting hook error is a nontrivial C6 logical operator. Every such
error has a nonzero syndrome and is rejected by the following SEC.
Combining the two-qubit C6 segment with a Q66 representative of weight at most
16 gives a weight-18 cat state for the joint
YC6​YQ66Y_{\mathrm{C6}}Y_{\mathrm{Q66}} observable in the double-RY​(π/4)R_{Y}(\pi/4)
injection.
Each Q66 cat-state factory contains two cat-state registers and one
verification bank, all provisioned at width 16. Increasing one factory’s width to
18 therefore requires 3​(18−16)=63(18-16)=6 additional qubits. We keep a six-qubit
C6 Bell-state source next to each C6 block. For C6 state verification, two
of these qubits form the Bell state described above. During the joint
YC6​YQ66Y_{\mathrm{C6}}Y_{\mathrm{Q66}} measurement, however, all six qubits are
loaned to the weight 16 Q66 cat-state factory so it may create
a weight 18 cat state. These two operations do not overlap, so
the same C6 Bell-state source can support both.
The logical measurement still contains 19 data–cat
interactions, so the logical-measurement error estimate below uses effective
width weff=19w_{\mathrm{eff}}=19.
At weff=19w_{\mathrm{eff}}=19 and p=10−4p=10^{-4}, the fitted EDM model of
Ref. [78] gives the single-round outcome-flip
probability
pflip≃2.1​weff​p=3.99×10−3.p_{\mathrm{flip}}\simeq 2.1w_{\mathrm{eff}}p=3.99\times 10^{-3}.
(13)
It takes three EDM rounds to suppress the measurement-induced error to the same
order of magnitude as the state preparation error (pH2p_{H^{2}}),
since pflip2=1.59×10−5p_{\mathrm{flip}}^{2}=1.59\times 10^{-5} whereas
pflip3=6.35×10−8p_{\mathrm{flip}}^{3}=6.35\times 10^{-8}. We therefore use EDM-3 for each
RY​(π/4)R_{Y}(\pi/4) injection. Its logical-outcome error rate is
pEDM3≃pflip3=6.35×10−8.p_{\mathrm{EDM3}}\simeq p_{\mathrm{flip}}^{3}=6.35\times 10^{-8}.
(14)
An EDM-3 outcome sequence is accepted when its three outcomes are all correct
or all flipped. The two inputs to a double injection come from the two
dedicated C6 blocks of the same Eastinthillation factory. Each input undergoes three
C6 SECs, and the factory accepts only if both C6 blocks accept all three.
Using the common C6 SEC rejection rate
rSECC6=0.323%r_{\mathrm{SEC}}^{\mathrm{C6}}=0.323\%, the per-injection acceptance
factor is therefore
aRY=[(1−pflip)3+pflip3]​(1−rSECC6)3=0.978534.a_{R_{Y}}=\bigl[(1-p_{\mathrm{flip}})^{3}+p_{\mathrm{flip}}^{3}\bigr](1-r_{\mathrm{SEC}}^{\mathrm{C6}})^{3}=0.978534.
(15)
The corresponding rejection rate is
rRY=1−aRY=2.147%r_{R_{Y}}=1-a_{R_{Y}}=2.147\%. The factor aRY8a_{R_{Y}}^{8} accounts for the
eight EDM-3 outcome sequences and the 24 C6 SECs required by one CCZ\mathrm{CCZ} preparation
attempt.
The parity check at the end of the Eastinthillation factory can only detect single-logical-qubit YY or XX errors; and all errors
in the synthillation protocol can effectively be traced to RY​(π/4)R_{Y}(\pi/4) injections. So we would like to ensure
that RY​(π/4)R_{Y}(\pi/4) injection errors are only either YY- or XX-type errors. We rely on the injected-YY noise model of the Walking Cat
architecture [78]: conditioned on the EDM and SEC
acceptance tests, an injection fault is represented by a stochastic logical
YY on the corresponding Q66 qubit. This follows from analyzing the
conditional corrections of the logical measurements involved in the RY​(π/4)R_{Y}(\pi/4) injection.
A wrong terminal DMX result erroneously applies a
YY correction. A wrong EDM result applies a
ZC6​RY​(π/2)Q66Z_{\mathrm{C6}}R_{Y}(\pi/2)_{\mathrm{Q66}} error; the extra
ZC6Z_{\mathrm{C6}} triggers the subsequent C6 XX readout and therefore
also applies a YY error via DMX YY correction (so, at the very least, is always correlated with a YY error).
The residual Q66 operation is
RY(π/2)Y≐RY(−π/2)R_{Y}(\pi/2)Y\doteq R_{Y}(-\pi/2), or the inverse rotation.
The source |H⟩\ket{H} state has zero YY expectation. The two outcomes of
the joint YC6​YQ66Y_{\mathrm{C6}}Y_{\mathrm{Q66}} measurement, and hence the two
residual rotations, are equiprobable. Averaging the corresponding channels
gives
12ℛY,π/2+12ℛY,−π/2=12ℐ+12𝒴,\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{R}_{Y,\pi/2}+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{R}_{Y,-\pi/2}=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{I}+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{Y},
(16)
where ℛY,θ\mathcal{R}_{Y,\theta} is the channel induced by RY​(θ)R_{Y}(\theta) and
𝒴\mathcal{Y} is conjugation by logical YY. We therefore bound the
stochastic logical-YY fault rate by the full EDM-3 logical-outcome error
probability.
The two input states in each injection layer have independent preparation
errors because they come from distinct C6 blocks. Because
faults at these locations are sampled independently, the
preparation errors are also independent. Consequently, for a
single injection, the marginal stochastic logical-YY probability is bounded
by the sum of the preparation and EDM-3 error probabilities:
pY,inj≲pH2+pEDM3=7.65×10−8.p_{Y,\mathrm{inj}}\lesssim p_{H^{2}}+p_{\mathrm{EDM3}}=7.65\times 10^{-8}.
(17)
Acceptance Measurement.
The acceptance measurement uses a LM1 operation mediated by Viterbi
measurement. Its representative has weight 16, so
wcat=16w_{\mathrm{cat}}=16, as shown in
Table 16. At p=10−4p=10^{-4}, the single-round
bit-flip model of Ref. [78] gives
pflip,16=2.1​wcat​p=2.1×16×10−4=3.36×10−3.p_{\mathrm{flip},16}=2.1w_{\mathrm{cat}}p=2.1\times 16\times 10^{-4}=3.36\times 10^{-3}.
(18)
For target error ε=10−11\varepsilon=10^{-11}, the Viterbi stopping condition
requires vote margin
d16=⌈log⁡[(1−ε)/ε]log⁡[(1−pflip,16)/pflip,16]⌉=5.d_{16}=\left\lceil\frac{\log[(1-\varepsilon)/\varepsilon]}{\log[(1-p_{\mathrm{flip},16})/p_{\mathrm{flip},16}]}\right\rceil=5.
(19)
The logical error is therefore
pVit,16=[1+(1−pflip,16pflip,16)5]−1=4.36×10−13.p_{\mathrm{Vit},16}=\left[1+\left(\frac{1-p_{\mathrm{flip},16}}{p_{\mathrm{flip},16}}\right)^{5}\right]^{-1}=4.36\times 10^{-13}.
(20)
The cat-state rejection model of Ref. [78] gives
pmiss,16=5​wcat​p=8.0×10−3p_{\mathrm{miss},16}=5w_{\mathrm{cat}}p=8.0\times 10^{-3}. Writing
ρ16=pflip,16/(1−pflip,16)\rho_{16}=p_{\mathrm{flip},16}/(1-p_{\mathrm{flip},16}), the average
duration is
τVit,16avg=11−pmiss,16​d161−2​pflip,16​1−ρ16d161+ρ16d16=5.07​SEC.\tau_{\mathrm{Vit},16}^{\mathrm{avg}}=\frac{1}{1-p_{\mathrm{miss},16}}\frac{d_{16}}{1-2p_{\mathrm{flip},16}}\frac{1-\rho_{16}^{d_{16}}}{1+\rho_{16}^{d_{16}}}=5.07~\mathrm{SEC}.
(21)
CCZ\mathrm{CCZ} Injection.
The three CCZ\mathrm{CCZ} injection measurements follow the protocol of
Sec. XII.4. The largest joint representative has weight 36,
with weight 20 from the Q102 logical ZZ operator and weight 16 from the Q66
logical ZZ operator. We therefore use wcat=36w_{\mathrm{cat}}=36 for all three
measurements to deduce an upper bound on the logical error of the measurements.
At p=10−4p=10^{-4}, the single-round bit-flip probability is
pflip,36=2.1×36×10−4=7.56×10−3.p_{\mathrm{flip},36}=2.1\times 36\times 10^{-4}=7.56\times 10^{-3}.
(22)
For target error ε=10−10\varepsilon=10^{-10}, the Viterbi stopping condition
requires vote margin
d36=⌈log⁡[(1−ε)/ε]log⁡[(1−pflip,36)/pflip,36]⌉=5.d_{36}=\left\lceil\frac{\log[(1-\varepsilon)/\varepsilon]}{\log[(1-p_{\mathrm{flip},36})/p_{\mathrm{flip},36}]}\right\rceil=5.
(23)
The logical error per measurement is therefore
pVit,36=[1+(1−pflip,36pflip,36)5]−1=2.57×10−11.p_{\mathrm{Vit},36}=\left[1+\left(\frac{1-p_{\mathrm{flip},36}}{p_{\mathrm{flip},36}}\right)^{5}\right]^{-1}=2.57\times 10^{-11}.
(24)
The cat-state rejection model of Ref. [78] gives
pmiss,36=5​wcat​p=1.8×10−2p_{\mathrm{miss},36}=5w_{\mathrm{cat}}p=1.8\times 10^{-2}. Writing
ρ36=pflip,36/(1−pflip,36)\rho_{36}=p_{\mathrm{flip},36}/(1-p_{\mathrm{flip},36}), the average
duration of one Viterbi measurement is
τVit,36avg\displaystyle\tau_{\mathrm{Vit},36}^{\mathrm{avg}}
=11−pmiss,36​d361−2​pflip,36\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-p_{\mathrm{miss},36}}\frac{d_{36}}{1-2p_{\mathrm{flip},36}}
(25)
×1−ρ36d361+ρ36d36=5.16982​SEC.\displaystyle\times\frac{1-\rho_{36}^{d_{36}}}{1+\rho_{36}^{d_{36}}}=5.16982~\mathrm{SEC}.
The three measurements run in parallel, so their duration is set by the
slowest measurement. Let F⁡(t)F(t) be the probability that one weight-36
Viterbi measurement has halted by SEC tt. We obtain F⁡(t)F(t) by iterating
the same vote-margin process used above. A cat-state attempt is rejected with
probability pmiss,36p_{\mathrm{miss},36}; it then yields no measurement outcome,
so the vote margin is unchanged in that SEC. Since the three measurements use
independent cat states, the probability that all three have halted by SEC
tt is F​(t)3F(t)^{3}. Summing the probability that at least one measurement is
still running after SEC tt gives
τCCZavg=∑t=0∞[1−F​(t)3]=5.46218​SEC.\tau_{\mathrm{CCZ}}^{\mathrm{avg}}=\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\left[1-F(t)^{3}\right]=5.46218~\mathrm{SEC}.
(26)
This is only 0.29​SEC0.29~\mathrm{SEC} longer than the mean duration of one
measurement. A union bound gives a logical-error contribution of at most
3​pVit,36=7.70×10−113p_{\mathrm{Vit},36}=7.70\times 10^{-11} from the three measurements.
Phase Fixups.
After the three CCZ\mathrm{CCZ} injection measurements, we measure the three commuting
resource-block observables P0,P1,P2P_{0},P_{1},P_{2} obtained by conjugating the
logical-XX operators by the tracked Clifford frame. This frame includes one
conditional Clifford correction from each of the eight RY​(π/4)R_{Y}(\pi/4)
injections, so we analyze all 28=2562^{8}=256 frame branches.
We call a destructive readout that measures each physical data qubit in an
independently chosen Pauli basis a generalized destructive measurement
(DMG). A single DMG measures logical Pauli observables with pairwise-disjoint
physical representatives in parallel: on the support of each representative,
the physical qubits are measured in the corresponding single-qubit Pauli
bases. A DMG takes only one POC–less than a full SEC.
Pairwise-disjoint representatives of all three PiP_{i} are not available on
every branch. When they are available, a single DMG measures the three PiP_{i}
in parallel. If exactly one pair overlaps, we measure one member of that pair
using a single Viterbi measurement.
We then measure the other two observables, whose
representatives are disjoint, in parallel with a single DMG. If every
pair overlaps, we measure products of the PiP_{i} that admit
disjoint representatives. In both cases requiring nondestructive logical measurement layers,
only one such layer precedes the terminal DMG. The phase fixups therefore
require at most one measurement layer.
We describe the MECM and Viterbi cases using the
scheduler-matrix convention of Ref. [81]: for
G∈𝔽23×3G\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{3\times 3}, column jj specifies the observable
Qj=∏i=02PiGi​j.Q_{j}=\prod_{i=0}^{2}P_{i}^{G_{ij}}.
(27)
If the eigenvalues of PiP_{i} and QjQ_{j} are (−1)ui(-1)^{u_{i}} and
(−1)vj(-1)^{v_{j}}, respectively, then
v=u​G,u=v​G−1.v=uG,\qquad u=vG^{-1}.
(28)
An invertible GG therefore replaces the PiP_{i} with an equivalent set of
logical observables QjQ_{j}. Measuring the QjQ_{j} gives the same logical
information because uu can be recovered from vv, and it requires no
additional measurement layer.
Only two scheduler matrices are required:
Gid=(100010001),Gprod=(101010001).G_{\mathrm{id}}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad G_{\mathrm{prod}}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&1\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}.
(29)
GidG_{\mathrm{id}} measures Qi=PiQ_{i}=P_{i} – it is no different from an ordinary Viterbi measurement.
On the 32 branches whose original generators ordinarily require three
measurement layers, GprodG_{\mathrm{prod}} replaces P2P_{2} with the equivalent
generator P0​P2P_{0}P_{2}, so that it measures
(Q0,Q1,Q2)=(P0,P1,P0​P2).(Q_{0},Q_{1},Q_{2})=(P_{0},P_{1},P_{0}P_{2}).
(30)
Since Gprod−1=GprodG_{\mathrm{prod}}^{-1}=G_{\mathrm{prod}}, the original outcomes are
u0=v0,u1=v1,u2=v0+v2(mod2).u_{0}=v_{0},\qquad u_{1}=v_{1},\qquad u_{2}=v_{0}+v_{2}\pmod{2}.
(31)
.

frame branches. On 32 branches, the three QjQ_{j} share a physical

other 224 branches, one QjQ_{j} is measured by a Viterbi measurement before the
remaining pair is obtained in parallel with a single DMG. On the branches using
Q2=P0​P2Q_{2}=P_{0}P_{2}, Q1Q_{1} and Q2Q_{2} have compatible, disjoint physical representatives
even though the three original generators cannot be partitioned into fewer

can be measured before the DMG layer. Physical representatives

XII.5.3 Analysis
Per-attempt failure rate.
A CCZ\mathrm{CCZ} preparation attempt accepts only if its eight RY​(π/4)R_{Y}(\pi/4) injections,
and final input-parity check all accept. From
eq. 15, the rejection probability of one
injection is rRY=1−aRY=2.147%r_{R_{Y}}=1-a_{R_{Y}}=2.147\%. The final parity check rejects an
odd number of injection faults, with probability
rpar\displaystyle r_{\mathrm{par}}
=∑j​odd(8j)​pY,injj​(1−pY,inj)8−j\displaystyle=\sum_{j\ \mathrm{odd}}\binom{8}{j}p_{Y,\mathrm{inj}}^{j}(1-p_{Y,\mathrm{inj}})^{8-j}
(36)
=1−(1−2​pY,inj)82≲6.12×10−7.\displaystyle=\frac{1-(1-2p_{Y,\mathrm{inj}})^{8}}{2}\lesssim 6.12\times 10^{-7}.
(37)
The per-attempt acceptance and failure probabilities therefore satisfy
1−pfailEastin\displaystyle 1-p_{\mathrm{fail}}^{\mathrm{Eastin}}
≳(1−rRY)8​(1−rpar)=84.06%,\displaystyle\gtrsim(1-r_{R_{Y}})^{8}(1-r_{\mathrm{par}})=84.06\%,
pfailEastin\displaystyle p_{\mathrm{fail}}^{\mathrm{Eastin}}
≲15.94%.\displaystyle\lesssim 15.94\%.
(38)
Factory depth.
We first compute Tprep(0)T_{\mathrm{prep}}^{(0)}, the POC depth required to
prepare one CCZ\mathrm{CCZ} state when the attempt detects no failure and the factory does
not restart. The superscript (0)(0) denotes this no-restart case. We obtain
this depth by counting the Q66 SECs in the preparation schedule and converting
that count to POCs. Preparing the Q66 resource block requires one logical zero
preparation (LZ), as defined in the Walking Cat instruction
set [78], four sequential pairs of RY​(π/4)R_{Y}(\pi/4)
injections, and the final acceptance measurement. LZ takes one Q66 SEC. Each
pair of rotations is performed in parallel by EDM-3 and therefore takes three Q66 SECs.
Once the final C6 SEC finishes, we prepare the input states for the next pair
of RY​(π/4)R_{Y}(\pi/4) injections in parallel with the final Q66 SEC. This
preparation takes only 12.0 POCs and therefore finishes before the next Q66 SEC
begins.
τVit,16avg=5.07\tau_{\mathrm{Vit},16}^{\mathrm{avg}}=5.07 SECs of the acceptance
measurement, the Q66 block is occupied for an average of 18.07 Q66 SECs
during preparation. Finally, as a Q66 SEC has POC depth
TSECQ66=17T_{\mathrm{SEC}}^{\mathrm{Q66}}=17:
NSECprep\displaystyle N_{\mathrm{SEC}}^{\mathrm{prep}}
=1+4×3+5.07=18.07,\displaystyle=1+4\times 3+5.07=18.07,
Tprep(0)\displaystyle T_{\mathrm{prep}}^{(0)}
=NSECprep​TSECQ66\displaystyle=N_{\mathrm{SEC}}^{\mathrm{prep}}T_{\mathrm{SEC}}^{\mathrm{Q66}}
=18.07​SEC×17​POCsSEC\displaystyle=18.07~\mathrm{SEC}\times 17~\frac{\mathrm{POCs}}{\mathrm{SEC}}
=307.19​POCs.\displaystyle=307.19~\mathrm{POCs}.
(39)
The three CCZ\mathrm{CCZ} injection measurements are performed in parallel. Their joint
average duration is τCCZavg≈5.46\tau_{\mathrm{CCZ}}^{\mathrm{avg}}\approx 5.46 Q102 SECs from
eq. 26.
Tinj=τCCZavg​TSECQ102=147.48​POCs.T_{\mathrm{inj}}=\tau_{\mathrm{CCZ}}^{\mathrm{avg}}T_{\mathrm{SEC}}^{\mathrm{Q102}}=147.48~\mathrm{POCs}.
(40)
The phase-fixup schedule uses an average of 4.44 Q66 SECs, followed by the
one-POC terminal DMG. Its average depth is
Tphase=N¯SECphase​TSECQ66+1=4.44×17+1=76.48​POCs.T_{\mathrm{phase}}=\overline{N}_{\mathrm{SEC}}^{\mathrm{phase}}T_{\mathrm{SEC}}^{\mathrm{Q66}}+1=4.44\times 17+1=76.48~\mathrm{POCs}.
(41)
The depth from LZ through release of the Q66 block–assuming no restarts–is therefore
Tfactory(0)=Tprep(0)+Tinj+Tphase=531.15​POCs.T_{\mathrm{factory}}^{(0)}=T_{\mathrm{prep}}^{(0)}+T_{\mathrm{inj}}+T_{\mathrm{phase}}=531.15~\mathrm{POCs}.
(42)
Next, we account for the depth added from restarting the protocol in the event of
a detected error. Each of the four
RY​(π/4)R_{Y}(\pi/4)-injection layers performs two EDM-3 measurements in parallel.
An error may be detected after each of the three associated SECs or after the
final acceptance measurement. Let k∈{1,2,3,4}k\in\{1,2,3,4\} index the injection
layer and let j∈{1,2,3}j\in\{1,2,3\} index its SEC. The accumulated depth after SEC
jj of layer kk is
dk,j=1+3​(k−1)+j.d_{k,j}=1+3(k-1)+j.
(43)
The three injection checkpoints therefore occur at depths
(2,3,4)(2,3,4), (5,6,7)(5,6,7), (8,9,10)(8,9,10), and (11,12,13)(11,12,13) Q66 SECs for
layers one through four. The final acceptance measurement reports a failure
at depth
dpar=1+4×3+τVit,16avg=18.07​SECs.d_{\mathrm{par}}=1+4\times 3+\tau_{\mathrm{Vit},16}^{\mathrm{avg}}=18.07~\mathrm{SECs}.
(44)
Next, we calculate the error-detection probability after each SEC of the two
parallel EDM-3 measurements in a RY​(π/4)R_{Y}(\pi/4)-injection layer. After the
first SEC, a restart occurs only if either C6 SEC reports an error.
After the second and third SECs, a restart occurs if either C6 SEC reports an
error or if the EDM outcomes have a parity mismatch. We write the latter two
cases as disjoint events:
Pr⁡(restart at SEC)\displaystyle\Pr(\text{restart at SEC})
=Pr⁡(C6 SEC error)\displaystyle=\Pr(\text{C6 SEC error})
+Pr⁡(C6 SECs accept)\displaystyle\quad+\Pr(\text{C6 SECs accept})
×Pr⁡(EDM mismatch).\displaystyle\qquad\times\Pr(\text{EDM mismatch}).
(45)
An EDM mismatch cannot be detected after the first SEC because each EDM-3
sequence has only one outcome at that point. The second and third SECs can
detect a disagreement among the accumulated outcomes.
Let f=pflip=3.99×10−3f=p_{\mathrm{flip}}=3.99\times 10^{-3}. One EDM-3 sequence agrees through
mm SECs when all mm outcomes are correct or all mm outcomes are
flipped. The probability that both parallel EDM-3 sequences agree through
m∈{2,3}m\in\{2,3\} SECs is
um\displaystyle u_{m}
=[(1−f)m+fm]2,\displaystyle=\bigl[(1-f)^{m}+f^{m}\bigr]^{2},
u2\displaystyle u_{2}
=0.984167,u3=0.976298.\displaystyle=0.984167,\qquad u_{3}=0.976298.
(46)
The probability that both C6 SECs in one round accept is
c=(1−rSECC6)2=0.993550,1−c=0.00644957.c=(1-r_{\mathrm{SEC}}^{\mathrm{C6}})^{2}=0.993550,\qquad 1-c=0.00644957.
(47)
Here 1−c1-c is the probability that at least one C6 SEC reports an error.
Conditional on reaching a given SEC, the restart probabilities after the
first, second, and third SECs are
e1\displaystyle e_{1}
=1−c,\displaystyle=1-c,
e2\displaystyle e_{2}
=(1−c)+c⁡(1−u2)=1−c​u2,\displaystyle=(1-c)+c(1-u_{2})=1-cu_{2},
e3\displaystyle e_{3}
=(1−c)+c⁡(1−u3u2)=1−c​u3u2.\displaystyle=(1-c)+c\left(1-\frac{u_{3}}{u_{2}}\right)=1-c\frac{u_{3}}{u_{2}}.
(48)
The first term in each expression is the C6 SEC rejection probability. The
second term in e2e_{2} and e3e_{3} is the probability that both C6 SECs accept
but the EDM outcomes disagree.
Each injection layer requires two zero-level input-pair preparations. Both
preparations accept with probability
h=(1−rrej(0))2h=(1-r_{\mathrm{rej}}^{(0)})^{2}. If either preparation rejects, the
parallel preparation batch is repeated. The first batch has depth
Tdist(0)=12T_{\mathrm{dist}}^{(0)}=12 POCs and is hidden inside the 17-POC Q66
scheduling window. Each additional batch adds 12/1712/17 Q66 SECs. For the
geometrically distributed number of batches, the mean additional
critical-path depth per reached injection layer is
δH2=1217​(1h−1)=0.00508​Q66 SECs.\delta_{H^{2}}=\frac{12}{17}\left(\frac{1}{h}-1\right)=0.00508~\text{Q66 SECs}.
(49)
So, a C6 rejection delays the injection layer but does not
require a full-fledged factory restart. Once both inputs are available, a parallel injection layer completes
with probability
a=c3​u3=aRY2=0.957529.a=c^{3}u_{3}=a_{R_{Y}}^{2}=0.957529.
(50)
Conditional on starting the parallel EDM-3 measurements, the probabilities
that the attempt terminates after the first, second, or third SEC are
g1\displaystyle g_{1}
=e1,\displaystyle=e_{1},
g2\displaystyle g_{2}
=c​e2,\displaystyle=ce_{2},
g3\displaystyle g_{3}
=c2​u2​e3.\displaystyle=c^{2}u_{2}e_{3}.
(51)
These probabilities satisfy ∑j=13gj=1−a\sum_{j=1}^{3}g_{j}=1-a. Layer kk is reached
with probability ak−1a^{k-1}, so the unconditional restart probability at
checkpoint jj of that layer is ak−1​gja^{k-1}g_{j}.
The acceptance measurement is reached with probability a4a^{4} and detects
an injection fault with probability rparr_{\mathrm{par}}. Its unconditional
restart probability is
qpar=a4​rpar=5.14×10−7.q_{\mathrm{par}}=a^{4}r_{\mathrm{par}}=5.14\times 10^{-7}.
(52)
An attempt succeeds with probability
A=a4​(1−rpar)=0.840636.A=a^{4}(1-r_{\mathrm{par}})=0.840636.
(53)
The expected number of failures at each checkpoint per accepted state is the
unconditional checkpoint probability divided by AA. The average
preparation depth is therefore
N¯SECprep\displaystyle\overline{N}_{\mathrm{SEC}}^{\mathrm{prep}}
=NSECprep+1A[∑k=14ak−1(δH2+∑j=13gjdk,j)\displaystyle=N_{\mathrm{SEC}}^{\mathrm{prep}}+\frac{1}{A}\Biggl[\sum_{k=1}^{4}a^{k-1}\left(\delta_{H^{2}}+\sum_{j=1}^{3}g_{j}d_{k,j}\right)
+qpardpar]\displaystyle\qquad+q_{\mathrm{par}}d_{\mathrm{par}}\Biggr]
=18.07+1.44730=19.5173​SECs,\displaystyle=18.07+1.44730=19.5173~\mathrm{SECs},
T¯prep\displaystyle\overline{T}_{\mathrm{prep}}
=N¯SECprep​TSECQ66=331.79​POCs.\displaystyle=\overline{N}_{\mathrm{SEC}}^{\mathrm{prep}}T_{\mathrm{SEC}}^{\mathrm{Q66}}=331.79~\mathrm{POCs}.
(54)
The CCZ\mathrm{CCZ} injection and phase-fixup stages are executed only after the parity
check accepts. Their Viterbi repetition costs are already included in
eqs. 40 and 41. The complete
average depth per accepted and consumed CCZ\mathrm{CCZ} state is consequently
T¯factory=T¯prep+Tinj+Tphase=555.75​POCs.\overline{T}_{\mathrm{factory}}=\overline{T}_{\mathrm{prep}}+T_{\mathrm{inj}}+T_{\mathrm{phase}}=555.75~\mathrm{POCs}.
(55)
Factory footprint.
We say that a number of factories is sufficient to produce a continuous stream
of CCZ\mathrm{CCZ} states if, collectively, their average time to produce a magic state is
shorter than the average time per state injection.
The point-addition circuit executes at most one Toffoli gate at a time. A Q102
memory block occupies the CCZ\mathrm{CCZ} injection interface for
τCCZavg≈5.46\tau_{\mathrm{CCZ}}^{\mathrm{avg}}\approx 5.46 Q102 SECs and can therefore consume
at most one CCZ\mathrm{CCZ} state every Tinj=147.48T_{\mathrm{inj}}=147.48 POCs. Since one factory supplies an accepted state
every 555.75555.75 POCs on average, the number of factories required to maintain
this consumption rate is
NCCZ=⌈T¯factoryτCCZavg​TSECQ102⌉=⌈555.75147.48⌉=4.N_{\mathrm{CCZ}}=\left\lceil\frac{\overline{T}_{\mathrm{factory}}}{\tau_{\mathrm{CCZ}}^{\mathrm{avg}}T_{\mathrm{SEC}}^{\mathrm{Q102}}}\right\rceil=\left\lceil\frac{555.75}{147.48}\right\rceil=4.
(56)
Each Eastinthillation factory uses one Q66 block, three weight-16 cat-state
factories, two dedicated zero-level C6 source blocks, and one six-qubit C6
Bell-state source per C6 block. As derived in Sec. E.1,
this gives 319 physical qubits per factory and 1,276 physical qubits for all
four factories.
The resulting layout is shown in
Fig. 20.
Figure 20: Layout of the four Eastinthillation factories required to sustain the
application consumption rate. Each Q66 block is adjacent to three
weight-16 cat-state factories and has two dedicated zero-level C6
TT-state factories. The two cyan sites at the lower left of each factory
are its local six-qubit C6 Bell-state sources.

pL,inEastin≲28​pY,inj2≲28​(7.65×10−8)2=1.64×10−13.p_{\mathrm{L,in}}^{\mathrm{Eastin}}\lesssim 28p_{Y,\mathrm{inj}}^{2}\lesssim 28\bigl(7.65\times 10^{-8}\bigr)^{2}=1.64\times 10^{-13}.
(57)
The Q66 logical error rate is
pL,SECQ66=2.63×10−11p_{\mathrm{L,SEC}}^{\mathrm{Q66}}=2.63\times 10^{-11} per SEC. The Q66

during preparation. During CCZ\mathrm{CCZ} injection, one Q102 SEC lasts
TSECQ102/TSECQ66=27/17T_{\mathrm{SEC}}^{\mathrm{Q102}}/T_{\mathrm{SEC}}^{\mathrm{Q66}}=27/17 Q66 SECs, and the phase-fixup schedule adds
N¯SECphase\overline{N}_{\mathrm{SEC}}^{\mathrm{phase}} Q66 SECs. A union

and Q66 SEC contributions gives
pLEastin\displaystyle p_{\mathrm{L}}^{\mathrm{Eastin}}

+(N¯SECprep+τCCZavg​TSECQ102TSECQ66+N¯SECphase)​pL,SECQ66\displaystyle\quad+\left(\overline{N}_{\mathrm{SEC}}^{\mathrm{prep}}+\tau_{\mathrm{CCZ}}^{\mathrm{avg}}\frac{T_{\mathrm{SEC}}^{\mathrm{Q102}}}{T_{\mathrm{SEC}}^{\mathrm{Q66}}}+\overline{N}_{\mathrm{SEC}}^{\mathrm{phase}}\right)p_{\mathrm{L,SEC}}^{\mathrm{Q66}}
(58)
=7.80×10−11\displaystyle=7.80\times 10^{-11}
+(19.5173+τCCZavg​2717CLOSE\displaystyle\quad+\left(19.5173+\tau_{\mathrm{CCZ}}^{\mathrm{avg}}\frac{27}{17}\right.
OPEN+4.44)​(2.63×10−11)\displaystyle\qquad\left.{}+4.44\right)(2.63\times 10^{-11})
=9.36×10−10.\displaystyle=9.36\times 10^{-10}.
(59)
Under the error model above, the logical error rate satisfies
pLEastin≲9.36×10−10.p_{\mathrm{L}}^{\mathrm{Eastin}}\lesssim 9.36\times 10^{-10}.
(60)
Q66 memory errors give the largest contribution to this bound by two orders of magnitude; the scheme is otherwise
capable of producing CCZ\mathrm{CCZ} gates with logical error rate of order 1e-13. This floor
can be met by higher-distance code or a decoder that
uses loss and leakage information, e.g. [54]. Or, one may exploit
soft information in the decoder: the factory can reject and
restart low-confidence attempts before their
outputs are used [8, 75].
XII.5.4 T-state factory conversion
The windowed semiclassical iQFT requires arbitrary-angle ZZ-rotations,
which can be synthesized approximately with TT gates using the mixing+fallback method [47].
We only need 16 ZZ-rotations per window if we execute them sequentially,
feedforward measurement results and calculate the angles from the accumulated measurement outcomes.
The total number of ZZ-rotations across all iQFTs is 464, with 0.57​log2⁡(1/ε)+8.830.57\log_{2}(1/\varepsilon)+8.83 TT gates per rotation,
where ε\varepsilon is the approximation error per rotation.
However, the Eastinthillation factory can prepare only CCZ\mathrm{CCZ} states. We can
repurpose its components to produce TT states instead.
The local C6 TT-state factories inject the required
TT states into the Q66 blocks, where they can be safely stored in memory.
The states are then teleported to the Q102
blocks as required. Using the measured logical error rate of
1.30×10−81.30\times 10^{-8} per accepted pair, and minimizing for the total diamond-norm distance,
we obtain the optimal ε=5.30×10−9\varepsilon=5.30\times 10^{-9} and the average TT-sequence length 24.5.
This results in an expected total of 5,684 required TT pairs.
This gives the total iQFT-stage failure probability, by the union bound, at most
piQFT≤5,684​(1.30×10−8)+464​(5.30×10−9)=\displaystyle p_{\mathrm{iQFT}}\leq 5{,}684\left(1.30\times 10^{-8}\right)+464\left(5.30\times 10^{-9}\right)=
=7.64×10−5.\displaystyle=7.64\times 10^{-5}.
(61)
Part VI Conclusion
We presented optimized quantum circuits for solving the ECDLP on 𝚜𝚎𝚌𝚙𝟸𝟻𝟼𝚔𝟷\mathtt{secp256k1} using Shor’s algorithm, derived rigorous bounds on the success probability of
the implementation—taking into account phase- and output-errors due to approximate arithmetic—and compiled our implementation to our application-specific extension of the Walking Cat architecture [78] using our compilation toolchain, allowing us to accurately account for the overheads due to layout and routing.
By combining quantum-circuit, compilation, and architecture improvements, we showed that a trapped-ion quantum computer with 20,00020{,}000 qubits should be able to solve the ECDLP on 𝚜𝚎𝚌𝚙𝟸𝟻𝟼𝚔𝟷\mathtt{secp256k1} within 26 days.
Because we intentionally prioritize simple, uniform implementations over exhaustive co-optimization, we expect future work to improve the runtime, physical-qubit footprint, and success probability reported here.
In our resource estimates, we have taken into account hardware details that have typically been ignored in the literature.
This enables us to be more confident about our runtime and footprint estimates.
For instance, one of the major issues with trapped-ion architectures (and one we prominently addressed in the original Walking Cat architecture) is a loss cascade.
When an ion is lost, if its well is merged with that of a second ion to perform a two-qubit gate, the electrode configuration will not be correct for optimal trapping.
This can result in substantial heating of the remaining ion, which can lead to ejection from the trap. The leakage and loss reduction units (LLRUs) developed in that architecture assume that this ejection happens 100%100\% of the time.
However, for sufficiently deep traps, this might not be the case. Here, we use a different loss propagation model in which the ion is not ejected but its final position after a split is randomized, and we develop a new LLRU to address this model of loss propagation. We expect that the physical situation is more nuanced, with heating and ejection considered using a detailed microarchitecture, but we leave this to future work.
Several of our techniques could also reduce the resource requirements of other fault-tolerant quantum algorithms, e.g., for chemistry, condensed matter physics, and optimization [2, 20].
In particular, the Clifford frame clearing using logical CliNR and our integrated routing can be included in the general-purpose Walking Cat architecture without any additional hardware requirements.
The increased logical-measurement parallelism obtained using non-overlapping cat-based measurements is straightforward to include in the Walking Cat architecture at the price of adjusting the block allocation to allow for increased cat state throughput in the vicinity of the target blocks.
Lastly, our fast CCZ implementation provides a 31×31\times speedup over a CCZ compiled into Clifford and TT gates.
We leave it to future work to quantify the impact of these optimizations on quantum computing applications beyond the ECDLP.

Appendix A Algorithms
A.1 Affine elliptic curve point addition
This section contains integer-valued representations of the algorithms that comprise the affine elliptic curve point addition circuits in our implementation. Algorithm 3 shows the classical precomputation of the initial accumulator point and the lookup tables for each window in the point addition loop shown in Algorithm 4. There are L=⌈n/w⌉L=\lceil n/w\rceil windows of size ww with the top window possibly containing less than ww bits. After sampling the random initialization point [a]​P[a]P by sampling a uniform random a∈ℤr∗a\in\mathbb{Z}_{r}^{*}, Algorithm 3 first computes the LL tables with multiples of the base point PP, then the LL tables with multiples of the point QQ of which we seek to compute the discrete logarithm with respect to base PP. It also computes and stores 3⋅x3\cdot x for the xx-coordinates of all points in the table.
Algorithm 3 Randomized window setup
Input: P,Q∈E⁡(𝔽p)P,Q\in E(\mathbb{F}_{p}) of prime order rr; width w≥1w\geq 1 with 2w<r2^{w}<r
Output: Accumulator A0A_{0} and classical augmented tables
(Ti)i=02​L−1(T_{i})_{i=0}^{2L-1}
1
a0←$ℤr∗a_{0}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{r}^{\ast}; A0←[a0]​PA_{0}\leftarrow[a_{0}]P
2
for i←0i\leftarrow 0 to L−1L-1 do
3
μi←$ℤr∖{−j​2i​wmodr:0≤j<2w}\mu_{i}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{r}\setminus\{-j2^{iw}\bmod r:0\leq j<2^{w}\}
4
for j←0j\leftarrow 0 to 2w−12^{w}-1 do
5
(xi,j,yi,j)←[j​2i​w+μi]​P(x_{i,j},y_{i,j})\leftarrow[j2^{iw}+\mu_{i}]P
6
Ti​[j]←(xi,j,yi,j,3​xi,j)T_{i}[j]\leftarrow(x_{i,j},y_{i,j},3x_{i,j})
7
for i←Li\leftarrow L to 2​L−12L-1 do
8
μi←$ℤr∖{−j​2(i−L)​wmodr:0≤j<2w}\mu_{i}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{r}\setminus\{-j2^{(i-L)w}\bmod r:0\leq j<2^{w}\}
9
for j←0j\leftarrow 0 to 2w−12^{w}-1 do
10
(xi,j,yi,j)←[j​2(i−L)​w+μi]​Q(x_{i,j},y_{i,j})\leftarrow[j2^{(i-L)w}+\mu_{i}]Q
11
Ti​[j]←(xi,j,yi,j,3​xi,j)T_{i}[j]\leftarrow(x_{i,j},y_{i,j},3x_{i,j})
12
return A0,(Ti)i=02​L−1A_{0},(T_{i})_{i=0}^{2L-1}
Algorithm 4 is a simple loop over all 2​L2L windows that just adds the respective table points. Note that the scalars kk and ll are decomposed into their windowed 2w2^{w}-ary representations
k=∑i=0L−1ki​2i​w,l=∑i=0L−1li​2i​w,k=\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}k_{i}2^{iw},\quad l=\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}l_{i}2^{iw},
and the digits kik_{i} and lil_{i} are used to look up the correct table points that are passed to the affine addition AffineAdd\mathrm{AffineAdd} depicted in Algorithm 5. In practice, we use w=16w=16 and we only perform 2828 of the 3232 point additions as described in [51]: We replace the first point addition by a table lookup and drop the final 33 windowed point additions in favor of extended classical postprocessing [26]. We start with the ww most-significant bits of the first scalar and move toward the least-significant bit in each step, implementing the semi-classical quantum Fourier transform in blocks of size ww [34].
Algorithm 4 Windowed double-scalar multiplication
Input: Registers k,lk,l; accumulator A=A0A=A_{0} and tables
(Ti)i=02​L−1(T_{i})_{i=0}^{2L-1} from Algorithm 3
Output: k,lk,l unchanged; updated accumulator AA
1
for i←0i\leftarrow 0 to L−1L-1 do
2
A←𝖠𝖿𝖿𝗂𝗇𝖾𝖠𝖽𝖽⁡(A,ki,Ti)A\leftarrow\mathsf{AffineAdd}(A;k_{i},T_{i})
3
for i←Li\leftarrow L to 2​L−12L-1 do
4
A←𝖠𝖿𝖿𝗂𝗇𝖾𝖠𝖽𝖽⁡(A,li−L,Ti)A\leftarrow\mathsf{AffineAdd}(A;l_{i-L},T_{i})
5
return k,l,Ak,l,A
The affine addition algorithm 𝖠𝖿𝖿𝗂𝗇𝖾𝖠𝖽𝖽\mathsf{AffineAdd} uses the following subroutines.
𝖡𝗂𝗇𝖺𝗋𝗒𝖦𝖢𝖣𝖱𝖾𝖼𝗈𝗋𝖽\mathsf{BinaryGCDRecord} consumes its input and retains the
compressed Dialog/add-sub record.
𝖡𝗂𝗇𝖺𝗋𝗒𝖦𝖢𝖣𝖨𝗇𝗏𝖬𝗎𝗅\mathsf{BinaryGCDInvMul} and
𝖡𝗂𝗇𝖺𝗋𝗒𝖦𝖢𝖣𝖬𝗎𝗅\mathsf{BinaryGCDMul} replay it to compute the in-place division or in-place multiplication, respectively, and
𝖡𝗂𝗇𝖺𝗋𝗒𝖦𝖢𝖣𝖴𝗇𝗋𝖾𝖼𝗈𝗋𝖽\mathsf{BinaryGCDUnrecord} restores the input and clears all
record scratch. The two replays use modular halvings and doublings,
respectively.
The notation 𝖫𝗈𝗈𝗄𝗎𝗉(x,y)\mathsf{Lookup}_{(x,y)} or 𝖫𝗈𝗈𝗄𝗎𝗉3​x\mathsf{Lookup}_{3x} loads the indicated
coordinates, while m←𝖷𝖢𝗅𝖾𝖺𝗋⁡(u)m\leftarrow\mathsf{XClear}(u) measures and clears a lookup
register and records its XX-basis outcome. The final
𝖫𝗈𝗈𝗄𝗎𝗉𝖯𝗁𝖺𝗌𝖾𝖥𝗂𝗑\mathsf{LookupPhaseFix} applies the deferred lookup correction. Displayed
modular additions, subtractions, and the square-subtract use the approximate circuits. The square-subtract circuit is the one-split Karatsuba version specific
for 𝚜𝚎𝚌𝚙𝟸𝟻𝟼𝚔𝟷\mathtt{secp256k1}.
Note that we classically sample a uniform random mask R∈𝔽pR\in\mathbb{F}_{p} and add it with an approximate quantum-classical adder to the xx-register before the lookup of the xx-coordinate multiple. This randomizes the accumulator register in the square-sub circuit. The mask is removed after the subtraction of the square by an approximate addition of −Rmodp-R\bmod p. The separate addition step x←(x+R)modpx\leftarrow(x+R)\bmod p can be omitted if a fresh offset RiR_{i} is sampled for the ii-th window in Algorithm 3 during the generation of the lookup tables. The table would then contain 3​xi,j+Ri3x_{i,j}+R_{i} instead of 3​xi,j3x_{i,j} and the masked value would be looked up, making the separate addition unnecessary.
Algorithm 5 𝖠𝖿𝖿𝗂𝗇𝖾𝖠𝖽𝖽\mathsf{AffineAdd}: affine addition with direct-value
binary-gcd arithmetic
Input: Accumulator (x,y)=P1(x,y)=P_{1}; selector jj; table TT with
T⁡[j]=(x2,y2,3​x2)T[j]=(x_{2},y_{2},3x_{2}) and P2=(x2,y2)≠𝒪P_{2}=(x_{2},y_{2})\neq\mathcal{O}
Output: On success, (x,y)=P1+P2(x,y)=P_{1}+P_{2}; jj unchanged
1
(x2,y2)←𝖫𝗈𝗈𝗄𝗎𝗉(x,y)​(T,j)(x_{2},y_{2})\leftarrow\mathsf{Lookup}_{(x,y)}(T,j)
2
x←(x−x2)modpx\leftarrow(x-x_{2})\bmod p
3
mx(1)←𝖷𝖢𝗅𝖾𝖺𝗋⁡(x2)m_{x}^{(1)}\leftarrow\mathsf{XClear}(x_{2})
4
y←(y−y2)modpy\leftarrow(y-y_{2})\bmod p
5
my(1)←𝖷𝖢𝗅𝖾𝖺𝗋⁡(y2)m_{y}^{(1)}\leftarrow\mathsf{XClear}(y_{2})
6
ρ←𝖡𝗂𝗇𝖺𝗋𝗒𝖦𝖢𝖣𝖱𝖾𝖼𝗈𝗋𝖽⁡(x)\rho\leftarrow\mathsf{BinaryGCDRecord}(x)
7
y←𝖡𝗂𝗇𝖺𝗋𝗒𝖦𝖢𝖣𝖨𝗇𝗏𝖬𝗎𝗅⁡(ρ,y)y\leftarrow\mathsf{BinaryGCDInvMul}(\rho,y)
8
x←𝖡𝗂𝗇𝖺𝗋𝗒𝖦𝖢𝖣𝖴𝗇𝗋𝖾𝖼𝗈𝗋𝖽⁡(ρ)x\leftarrow\mathsf{BinaryGCDUnrecord}(\rho)
9
R←$𝔽pR\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{F}_{p}
10
x←(x+R)modpx\leftarrow(x+R)\bmod p
11
x2←𝖫𝗈𝗈𝗄𝗎𝗉3​x​(T,j)x_{2}\leftarrow\mathsf{Lookup}_{3x}(T,j)
12
x←(x+x2)modpx\leftarrow(x+x_{2})\bmod p
13
mx(2)←𝖷𝖢𝗅𝖾𝖺𝗋⁡(x2)m_{x}^{(2)}\leftarrow\mathsf{XClear}(x_{2})
14
x←(x−y2)modpx\leftarrow(x-y^{2})\bmod p
15
x←(x+(−Rmodp))modpx\leftarrow(x+(-R\bmod p))\bmod p
16
ρ←𝖡𝗂𝗇𝖺𝗋𝗒𝖦𝖢𝖣𝖱𝖾𝖼𝗈𝗋𝖽⁡(x)\rho\leftarrow\mathsf{BinaryGCDRecord}(x)
17
y←𝖡𝗂𝗇𝖺𝗋𝗒𝖦𝖢𝖣𝖬𝗎𝗅⁡(ρ,y)y\leftarrow\mathsf{BinaryGCDMul}(\rho,y)
18
x←𝖡𝗂𝗇𝖺𝗋𝗒𝖦𝖢𝖣𝖴𝗇𝗋𝖾𝖼𝗈𝗋𝖽⁡(ρ)x\leftarrow\mathsf{BinaryGCDUnrecord}(\rho)
19
(x2,y2)←𝖫𝗈𝗈𝗄𝗎𝗉(x,y)​(T,j)(x_{2},y_{2})\leftarrow\mathsf{Lookup}_{(x,y)}(T,j)

27
return x,y,jx,y,j
A.2 Modular subtraction of a square
This section provides explicit integer-valued algorithm descriptions for the operation that subtracts a square modulo pp, i.e., |x⟩​|y⟩\ket{x}\ket{y} to |x⟩​|(y−x2)modp⟩\ket{x}\ket{(y-x^{2})\bmod p}. The main algorithm is Algorithm 6. It uses 𝖠𝖽𝖽𝖡𝖬𝗎𝗅𝗍𝗂𝗉𝗅𝖾±q\mathsf{AddBMultiple}_{\pm}^{q} (Algorithm 9), 𝖠𝖽𝖽𝖢𝖬𝗎𝗅𝗍𝗂𝗉𝗅𝖾−q\mathsf{AddCMultiple}_{-}^{q} (Algorithm 10), and 𝖠𝖽𝖽𝖲𝗅𝗂𝖼𝖾σq\mathsf{AddSlice}_{\sigma}^{q} (Algorithm 7), which in turn uses 𝖯𝗁𝖺𝗌𝖾𝖦𝖤q\mathsf{PhaseGE}^{q} (Algorithm 8) and is itself a component of 𝖠𝖽𝖽𝖡𝖬𝗎𝗅𝗍𝗂𝗉𝗅𝖾\mathsf{AddBMultiple} and 𝖠𝖽𝖽𝖢𝖬𝗎𝗅𝗍𝗂𝗉𝗅𝖾\mathsf{AddCMultiple}.
We treat input values as integers less than 2n2^{n} that are not necessarily canonical representatives less than pp. Intermediate results are also integer values of their respective bit sizes. The phase ϕ\phi is implicit in all algorithm signatures, except where it is displayed explicitly by 𝖯𝗁𝖺𝗌𝖾𝖦𝖤\mathsf{PhaseGE}. An assignment to ϕ\phi records the phase acquired by the active computational-basis branch.
For the specific parameters of the curve 𝚜𝚎𝚌𝚙𝟸𝟻𝟼𝚔𝟷\mathtt{secp256k1}, 𝖠𝖽𝖽𝖡𝖬𝗎𝗅𝗍𝗂𝗉𝗅𝖾±q\mathsf{AddBMultiple}_{\pm}^{q} and 𝖠𝖽𝖽𝖢𝖬𝗎𝗅𝗍𝗂𝗉𝗅𝖾−q\mathsf{AddCMultiple}_{-}^{q} use the signed binary representation of cc in the form of the list
Tc\displaystyle T_{c}
=((32,+1),(10,+1),(6,−1),(4,+1),(0,+1)),\displaystyle=((32,+1),(10,+1),(6,-1),(4,+1),(0,+1)),
c\displaystyle c
=∑(d,ϵ)∈Tcϵ​2d=232+210−26+24+1.\displaystyle=\sum_{(d,\epsilon)\in T_{c}}\epsilon 2^{d}=2^{32}+2^{10}-2^{6}+2^{4}+1.
Besides the given curve parameters nn and cc, the carry-propagation width κ\kappa, the local carry-padding width ρ\rho, and the phase-comparison width δ\delta are positive integers that can be selected for different trade-offs. We assume that ρ≥0\rho\geq 0, 1≤δ≤n21\leq\delta\leq\frac{n}{2}, κ≥1\kappa\geq 1, 2​c≤2κ≤2n2c\leq 2^{\kappa}\leq 2^{n}, 32+n2+2+ρ<n32+\frac{n}{2}+2+\rho<n, κ+ρ<n\kappa+\rho<n to ensure that all slice and window invocations in these operations are well-formed.
Algorithm 6 One-level Karatsuba square-subtract
Input: x,y∈ℤx,y\in\mathbb{Z}, 0≤x,y<p0\leq x,y<p, control q∈{0,1}q\in\{0,1\};
requested phase-comparison width δ∈ℤ≥1\delta\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1},
δ≤n/2\delta\leq n/2
Output: x,y←x,y′x,y\leftarrow x,y^{\prime}, where y′∈[0,p)y^{\prime}\in[0,p),
y′≡y−q​x2(modp)y^{\prime}\equiv y-qx^{2}\pmod{p} (on failure-free execution)
1
xL≡x[0,n/2)x_{L}\equiv x_{[0,n/2)}; xH≡x[n/2,n)x_{H}\equiv x_{[n/2,n)}
// register views; x=B​xH+xLx=Bx_{H}+x_{L}
2
v←xL2v\leftarrow x_{L}^{2} // exact integer square in nn-bit scratch
3
(v,y)←𝖠𝖽𝖽𝖡𝖬𝗎𝗅𝗍𝗂𝗉𝗅𝖾+q​(v,y,n,δ)(v,y)\leftarrow\mathsf{AddBMultiple}_{+}^{q}(v,y;n;\delta) // add B​xL2Bx_{L}^{2}
4
(v,y)←𝖠𝖽𝖽𝖲𝗅𝗂𝖼𝖾−q​(v,y,n,0,n,0,δ)(v,y)\leftarrow\mathsf{AddSlice}_{-}^{q}(v,y;n,0,n,0;\delta) // add −xL2-x_{L}^{2}
5
v←0v\leftarrow 0 // uncompute xL2x_{L}^{2}
6
v←xH2v\leftarrow x_{H}^{2} // exact integer square in nn-bit scratch
7
(v,y)←𝖠𝖽𝖽𝖡𝖬𝗎𝗅𝗍𝗂𝗉𝗅𝖾+q​(v,y,n,δ)(v,y)\leftarrow\mathsf{AddBMultiple}_{+}^{q}(v,y;n;\delta) // add B​xH2Bx_{H}^{2}
8
(v,y)←𝖠𝖽𝖽𝖢𝖬𝗎𝗅𝗍𝗂𝗉𝗅𝖾−q​(v,y,δ)(v,y)\leftarrow\mathsf{AddCMultiple}_{-}^{q}(v,y;\delta) // add −c​xH2-cx_{H}^{2}
9
v←0v\leftarrow 0 // uncompute xH2x_{H}^{2}
10
u←0u\leftarrow 0; x^H≡xH+u​B\widehat{x}_{H}\equiv x_{H}+uB
// append one clean bit; no copy of xHx_{H}
11
x^H←x^H+xL\widehat{x}_{H}\leftarrow\widehat{x}_{H}+x_{L}
// overwrite the high half of xx in place
12
v←x^H2v\leftarrow\widehat{x}_{H}^{2} // exact integer square in (n+2)(n+2)-bit scratch
13
(v,y)←𝖠𝖽𝖽𝖡𝖬𝗎𝗅𝗍𝗂𝗉𝗅𝖾−q​(v,y,n+2,δ)(v,y)\leftarrow\mathsf{AddBMultiple}_{-}^{q}(v,y;n+2;\delta)
// add −B​x^H2-B\widehat{x}_{H}^{2}
14
v←0v\leftarrow 0 // uncompute x^H2\widehat{x}_{H}^{2}
15
x^H←x^H−xL\widehat{x}_{H}\leftarrow\widehat{x}_{H}-x_{L}
// restore xHx_{H} and clear uu
16
release the clean bit uu
17
return x,yx,y

Appendix B Failure case analysis
As in essentially all previous work (see, e.g., [64, 35, 69, 33, 51, 3, 71]), the elliptic curve point addition circuits described here are approximate, which means there exist inputs for which the circuits fail to compute the correct result for reasons from two different categories. Failures in the first category occur when the input elliptic curve points constitute an exceptional case where the underlying addition formula fails. Failures in the second category are due to an approximate modular arithmetic component failing to produce the exact intended result. This section shows that the overall number of failure cases occurring in our proposed randomized circuit for affine point addition is small enough to allow the full Shor algorithm to succeed with high probability. In particular, we show that for each pair of scalars (k,l)(k,l), the probability that it leads to a failure case is bounded by a constant pfp_{f} independent of kk and ll.
B.1 Exceptional cases for elliptic curve point addition
As in most previous work, we use the affine point addition formula for the short Weierstrass model E:y2=x3+a​x+bE:y^{2}=x^{3}+ax+b. Given P1=(x1,y1)P_{1}=(x_{1},y_{1}) and P2=(x2,y2)P_{2}=(x_{2},y_{2}), the point P3=(x3,y3)=P1+P2P_{3}=(x_{3},y_{3})=P_{1}+P_{2} is computed as x3=λ2−x1−x2x_{3}=\lambda^{2}-x_{1}-x_{2} and y3=(x2−x3)​λ−y2y_{3}=(x_{2}-x_{3})\lambda-y_{2}, where λ=(y2−y1)/(x2−x1)\lambda=(y_{2}-y_{1})/(x_{2}-x_{1}). This formula has exceptional cases. It cannot be used if x2=x1x_{2}=x_{1}, i.e., if P1=±P2P_{1}=\pm P_{2}, and also if one of the points is the point at infinity 𝒪\mathcal{O}. Because quantum circuits can only implement reversible operations, the slope value λ\lambda is often uncomputed from the results by re-computing λ=(y2+y3)/(x2−x3)\lambda=(y_{2}+y_{3})/(x_{2}-x_{3}). This implies another exceptional case coming from the condition x3=x2x_{3}=x_{2}. It follows that y3=±y2y_{3}=\pm y_{2}, hence this happens when the sum is equal to ±P2\pm P_{2}, so either P1=𝒪P_{1}=\mathcal{O} or P1=−[2]​P2P_{1}=-[2]P_{2}. In total, there are four exceptional cases for the point P1P_{1} when we aim to add a point P2P_{2}, namely P1∈{𝒪,P2,−P2,−[2]​P2}P_{1}\in\{\mathcal{O},P_{2},-P_{2},-[2]P_{2}\}. Note that Roetteler et al. missed this fourth exceptional case in their analysis of the exceptional case count in [69, Section 4.2].
Let AA be the accumulator point represented by the quantum register that will hold the approximate superposition over (k,l)(k,l) of f⁡(k,l)=[k]​P+[l]​Qf(k,l)=[k]P+[l]Q after the computation. As already suggested in [64], we initialize it with a classically precomputed uniform random point [a0]​P[a_{0}]P, where a0a_{0} is sampled uniformly at random with a0∈ℤr∗=ℤr∖{0}a_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}_{r}^{*}=\mathbb{Z}_{r}\setminus\{0\}. Using this random offset allows us to avoid the exceptional case of adding a point to 𝒪\mathcal{O} and to reason about probabilities over the uniform random starting point. After the quantum double-scalar multiplication, the accumulator register holds an approximate superposition over kk and ll of f⁡(k,l)+[a0]​Pf(k,l)+[a_{0}]P. Since a0a_{0} is independent of (k,l)(k,l), the constant shift has no impact on the Fourier-basis measurement that follows and does not have to be subtracted from AA.
As described earlier, the double-scalar multiplication is computed via windowed addition of points looked up from precomputed tables of classical elliptic curve points. We fix a window size w>0w>0. The scalars are decomposed into windows of size ww as k=∑i=0L−1ki​2i⋅wk=\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}k_{i}2^{i\cdot w} and l=∑i=0L−1li​2i⋅wl=\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}l_{i}2^{i\cdot w} with ki,li∈{0,1,…,2w−1}k_{i},l_{i}\in\{0,1,\dots,2^{w}-1\}. There are 2​L2L separate tables of size 2w2^{w}, one for each window index 0≤i<2​L0\leq i<2L containing the precomputed classical multiples [j​2i​w]​P[j2^{iw}]P for all j∈{0,1,…,2w−1}j\in\{0,1,\dots,2^{w}-1\} when 0≤i<L0\leq i<L, and [j​2(i−L)​w]​Q[j2^{(i-L)w}]Q when L≤i<2​LL\leq i<2L. All tables contain the point 𝒪\mathcal{O} for the cases where one of the ki=0k_{i}=0 or one of the li=0l_{i}=0. Usually, the special case when the lookup point is 𝒪\mathcal{O} needs to be treated separately to ensure that the point addition circuit does not change the accumulator, leading to operations that are controlled on the condition ki=0k_{i}=0 (or li=0l_{i}=0). To avoid these controlled operations, we add a fixed classical masking point MiM_{i} to all points in the ii-th table. During the classical table generation, the point is chosen uniformly at random via sampling a uniform random μi∈ℤr\mu_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{r} and computing Mi=[μi]​PM_{i}=[\mu_{i}]P if 0≤i<L0\leq i<L (or Mi=[μi]​QM_{i}=[\mu_{i}]Q if L≤i<2​LL\leq i<2L) under the additional condition that none of [j​2i​w]​P+Mi[j2^{iw}]P+M_{i} (or [j​2(i−L)​w]​Q+Mi[j2^{(i-L)w}]Q+M_{i}) are equal to 𝒪\mathcal{O}, i.e., we sample μi∈ℤr∖{−2i​w​j∣j∈{0,1,…,2w−1}}\mu_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{r}\setminus\{-2^{iw}j\mid j\in\{0,1,\dots,2^{w}-1\}\} (or μi∈ℤr∖{−2(i−L)​w​j∣j∈{0,1,…,2w−1}}\mu_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{r}\setminus\{-2^{(i-L)w}j\mid j\in\{0,1,\dots,2^{w}-1\}\}). Since we assume that 2w<r2^{w}<r, there exists a point MiM_{i} for each table such that none of the shifted points is 𝒪\mathcal{O}. To summarize, the classical table TiT_{i} for looking up the point to be added into the accumulator for the ii-th window is given as
Ti={{[j​2i​w+μi]​P∣j∈[0,2w)}, if ​0≤i<L,{[j​2(i−L)​w+μi]​Q∣j∈[0,2w)}, if ​L≤i<2​L.T_{i}=\begin{cases}\{[j2^{iw}+\mu_{i}]P\mid j\in[0,2^{w})\},\text{ if }0\leq i<L,\\
\{[j2^{(i-L)w}+\mu_{i}]Q\mid j\in[0,2^{w})\},\text{ if }L\leq i<2L.\end{cases}
Using these tables results in another additive shift for the oracle computation. Due to random initialization and table masking, after the double scalar multiplication, the accumulator register holds an approximation to the superposition over all (k,l)(k,l) of

μP=∑i=0L−1μi​ and ​μQ=∑i=L2​L−1μi.\displaystyle\mu_{P}=\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\mu_{i}\text{ and }\mu_{Q}=\sum_{i=L}^{2L-1}\mu_{i}.
The shift [a0+μP]​P+[μQ]​Q[a_{0}+\mu_{P}]P+[\mu_{Q}]Q is independent of kk and ll and has no impact on the subsequent phase estimation step in Shor’s algorithm. Therefore, it does not need to be subtracted or uncomputed. Let μ=(μ0,μ1,…,μ2​L−1)\mu=(\mu_{0},\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{2L-1}) and define

For detailed, integer-valued pseudo-code representations of these operations, see Algorithms 3, 4, and 5.
Next, we analyze when a pair (k,l)(k,l) leads to an exceptional case during the windowed point addition. Let AiA_{i} denote the state of the accumulator point during an ideal, correct computation before the ii-th windowed point addition, i.e. A0=[a0]​PA_{0}=[a_{0}]P, A1=[a0+k0+μ0]​PA_{1}=[a_{0}+k_{0}+\mu_{0}]P, AL=[a0+k+μP]​PA_{L}=[a_{0}+k+\mu_{P}]P, AL+1=[a0+k+μP]​P+[l0+μL]​QA_{L+1}=[a_{0}+k+\mu_{P}]P+[l_{0}+\mu_{L}]Q, etc. Note that for i>0i>0, the accumulator AiA_{i} can only assume the value 𝒪\mathcal{O} in a correct computation if, in the preceding step, Ai−1A_{i-1} was equal to the negative of the added table point, a case already covered as an exceptional case for the earlier step. Moreover, our random offset ensures that A0≠𝒪A_{0}\neq\mathcal{O}. This means that an exceptional point in an addition step that adds the table point CiC_{i} to AiA_{i} comes from one of the three cases Ai∈{Ci,−Ci,−[2]​Ci}A_{i}\in\{C_{i},-C_{i},-[2]C_{i}\}.
A pair (k,l)(k,l) leads to an exceptional point addition if there exists an index 0≤i<2​L0\leq i<2L such that Ai∈{Ci,−Ci,−[2]​Ci}A_{i}\in\{C_{i},-C_{i},-[2]C_{i}\}, where Ci=[ki​2i​w+μi]​PC_{i}=[k_{i}2^{iw}+\mu_{i}]P if 0≤i<L0\leq i<L and Ci=[li−L​2(i−L)​w+μi]​QC_{i}=[l_{i-L}2^{(i-L)w}+\mu_{i}]Q if L≤i<2​LL\leq i<2L. Define the set of all a0∈ℤr∗a_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}_{r}^{*} that lead to an exceptional case for given (k,l)(k,l) as follows:
𝒳w,μ(k,l)={a0∈ℤr∗∣∃i<2L,Ai∈{Ci,−Ci,−[2]Ci}}.\mathcal{X}_{w,\mu}(k,l)=\{a_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}_{r}^{*}\mid\exists i<2L,A_{i}\in\{C_{i},-C_{i},-[2]C_{i}\}\}.
Because the map a0↦[a0]​Pa_{0}\mapsto[a_{0}]P is a bijection ℤr→⟨P⟩\mathbb{Z}_{r}\rightarrow\langle P\rangle, each of Ci,−Ci,−[2]​CiC_{i},-C_{i},-[2]C_{i} can only be reached by AiA_{i} for fixed (k,l)(k,l) starting from a single value for a0a_{0}. Therefore, we have
|𝒳w,μ​(k,l)|≤3​(2​L)=6​L.|\mathcal{X}_{w,\mu}(k,l)|\leq 3(2L)=6L.
Let f¯w,a0,μ​(k,l)\bar{f}_{w,a_{0},\mu}(k,l) denote the output of the ww-windowed affine-addition circuit for fa0,μ​(k,l)f_{a_{0},\mu}(k,l) when every modular-arithmetic subroutine is exact. If a0∉𝒳w,μ​(k,l)a_{0}\notin\mathcal{X}_{w,\mu}(k,l), every addition along the ideal trajectory is non-exceptional. Correctness of the affine addition circuit on non-exceptional inputs and induction over the 2​L2L additions therefore give f¯w,a0,μ​(k,l)=fa0,μ​(k,l)\bar{f}_{w,a_{0},\mu}(k,l)=f_{a_{0},\mu}(k,l). It follows that {a0∈ℤr∗∣f¯w,a0,μ​(k,l)≠fa0,μ​(k,l)}⊆𝒳w,μ​(k,l)\{a_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}_{r}^{*}\mid\bar{f}_{w,a_{0},\mu}(k,l)\neq f_{a_{0},\mu}(k,l)\}\subseteq\mathcal{X}_{w,\mu}(k,l).
