=|𝒳w,μ​(k,l)|r−1≤6​Lr−1.\displaystyle=\frac{|\mathcal{X}_{w,\mu}(k,l)|}{r-1}\leq\frac{6L}{r-1}.
For our concrete parameters of 𝚜𝚎𝚌𝚙𝟸𝟻𝟼𝚔𝟷\mathtt{secp256k1}, this bound is negligibly small, smaller than 2−2482^{-248}.
In practice, we implement only 29<2​L=3229<2L=32 windows (the first using a simple table lookup, 28 using windowed point additions), but the expression above still holds as a conservative upper bound for the failure probability due to exceptional cases.
B.2 Approximate modular arithmetic failures
The second category of failures occurs because the modular arithmetic operations that are used in the affine point addition circuit are approximate operations themselves [71]. Again, there exist inputs for which the circuits fail to produce the expected results.
The modular arithmetic circuits presented here make use of the special shape of the pseudo-Mersenne base field prime pp. Several simplifications make modular arithmetic circuits more efficient but possibly lead to failures. For example, integer comparison with pp for modular reduction is replaced by comparison of the most significant bits and the correction addition that depends on the outcome of the comparison truncates the carry propagation. These optimizations are applied to the modular reduction operations in modular addition, subtraction, doubling, and halving circuits [71]. Similarly, truncated comparisons during phase corrections after measurement-based carry uncomputation can lead to phase failures even if the resulting values are correct.
Furthermore, Dialog-based in-place multiplication [45, 71] uses a non-worst-case bound for both the number of iterations and the register sizes used as the algorithm progresses [64]. We begin by discussing the optimizations in modular reduction.
B.2.1 Approximate modular reduction
An element in 𝔽p\mathbb{F}_{p} is usually represented by its unique representative in {0,1,…,p−1}\{0,1,\dots,p-1\} (which we call the least non-negative or canonical representative). Integer arithmetic operations on such representatives may lead to values outside that range and a modular reduction is applied to reduce such a value back to its canonical representation.
For example, taking two input operands from 𝔽p\mathbb{F}_{p} and adding their standard integer representatives results in a value 0≤u<2​p−10\leq u<2p-1. Modular reduction then subtracts pp if u≥pu\geq p, which means umodp=uu\bmod p=u, if u<pu<p, and umodp=u−pu\bmod p=u-p if u≥pu\geq p. Thus, there are two computational components, namely, computing the integer comparison u<pu<p and depending on its outcome an integer subtraction u−pu-p.
We work in the specific setting of a pseudo-Mersenne prime pp of bitlength nn. Let c∈ℕc\in\mathbb{N} be odd, c≪2nc\ll 2^{n} such that p=2n−cp=2^{n}-c. That pp has nn bits implies 2​c≤2n2c\leq 2^{n}. For the curve 𝚜𝚎𝚌𝚙𝟸𝟻𝟼𝚔𝟷\mathtt{secp256k1}, we have n=256n=256 and c=232+29+28+27+26+24+1=232+977c=2^{32}+2^{9}+2^{8}+2^{7}+2^{6}+2^{4}+1=2^{32}+977. Subtracting such a pp results in u−p=u−(2n−c)=u+c−2nu-p=u-(2^{n}-c)=u+c-2^{n} and can be implemented by computing the addition u+cu+c and discarding the (n+1)(n+1)-th bit. Note that, if u≥pu\geq p, then u+c≥2nu+c\geq 2^{n} and its (n+1)(n+1)-th bit is always 11.
The two optimizations above, which were also used in previous work [71], lead to approximate modular reduction circuits that we use in different situations in our circuits. First, the full integer comparison u<pu<p is replaced by a comparison u<2nu<2^{n}, which amounts to simply observing the (n+1)(n+1)-th bit of uu. Instead of computing the integer comparison into a result bit that acts as the control for the modular subtraction, the (n+1)(n+1)-th bit directly serves as the control bit. This operation differs from exact integer comparison if
u∈[p,2n).u\in[p,2^{n}).
This means that for cc of the possible results of an addition, the optimized reduction fails to produce a canonical representative. Note that the result can still be represented with nn bits and is correct modulo pp but is by pp larger than the canonical representative. This behavior does not necessarily lead to a direct arithmetic failure; however, it might lead to failure cases when the larger result is used in further computations. We therefore treat non-canonical representatives for outputs of any operation as a failure case.
Second, for the addition u+cu+c, we truncate carry propagation after κ≤n\kappa\leq n bits for c<2κc<2^{\kappa} as in [71]. This operation differs from exact addition with full carry propagation if the carry propagates further than κ\kappa bits, i.e.,
umod2κ∈[2κ−c,2κ).u\bmod 2^{\kappa}\in[2^{\kappa}-c,2^{\kappa}).
This failure case, where the result is wrong modulo pp, occurs for a proportion of c/2κc/2^{\kappa} of input values, in which case the result after reduction is 2κ2^{\kappa} smaller than the correct one.
Because of the simplified comparison and since our circuits work on nn-bit quantum registers, we allow intermediate values modulo pp to be represented by integers in [0,2n)[0,2^{n}). We assume throughout that 2​c≤2κ≤2n2c\leq 2^{\kappa}\leq 2^{n}. For a non-negative integer uu, define
qu\displaystyle q_{u}
=⌊u/2n⌋,ru=u−qu​2n∈[0,2n),\displaystyle=\lfloor u/2^{n}\rfloor,\quad r_{u}=u-q_{u}2^{n}\in[0,2^{n}),
wu\displaystyle w_{u}
={1, if ​(rumod2κ)≥2κ−c,0, otherwise.\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1,\text{ if }\ (r_{u}\bmod 2^{\kappa})\geq 2^{\kappa}-c,\\
0,\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}
If u∈[0,2n+1)u\in[0,2^{n+1}), qu∈{0,1}q_{u}\in\{0,1\} is a single bit and the approximate reduction as described above then returns
redκ​(u)=ru+qu​c−qu​wu​2κ,\mathrm{red}_{\kappa}(u)=r_{u}+q_{u}c-q_{u}w_{u}2^{\kappa},
where quq_{u} is the (n+1)(n+1)-th bit, the correction is triggered if it is 11, in which case cc is added to the first nn bits of uu and the result is 2κ2^{\kappa} smaller than the correct value if the carry when adding cc propagates out of the first κ\kappa bits. Since the (n+1)(n+1)-th bit was discarded and the carry never propagates beyond the κ\kappa-th bit, redκ​(u)∈[0,2n)\mathrm{red}_{\kappa}(u)\in[0,2^{n}). Using 2n≡cmodp2^{n}\equiv c\bmod p and taking modulo pp gives
redκ​(u)≡u−qu​wu​2κ(modp),\mathrm{red}_{\kappa}(u)\equiv u-q_{u}w_{u}2^{\kappa}\pmod{p},
(62)
which means that the approximate reduction redκ\mathrm{red}_{\kappa} returns a correct integer representative of uu if and only if either no correction occurs (qu=0q_{u}=0) or the carry from the addition of cc does not propagate beyond κ\kappa bits (wu=0w_{u}=0). The above two failure cases imply incorrect result values. Another type of failure leads to an incorrect phase.
B.2.2 Approximate phase repair
To uncompute certain bits that are computed in our circuits such as a carry bit bb depending on a register |x⟩\ket{x}, we use measurement in the XX basis, which changes the phase by a factor (−1)m​b(-1)^{mb}, where mm is the measurement outcome. This means that a phase correction is required if m=1m=1 and b=1b=1. Our circuits use approximate recomputation of the bit bb for example by truncating carry propagation or comparing only the higher order bits. If the approximation is incorrect, a phase failure occurs because the circuit fails to correct the phase flip.
We now proceed to deduce bounds on the failure probabilities for the various components in the approximate affine point addition circuit. We start with the simpler modular operations such as addition, subtraction, and negation, then discuss the modular subtraction of a square, and finally, the in-place modular division and multiplication circuits.
B.2.3 Addition, subtraction, and negation
Addition.
The modular addition circuit we use [71] is an approximation of the operation |u⟩​|v⟩↦|u⟩​|(u+v)modp⟩\ket{u}\ket{v}\mapsto\ket{u}\ket{(u+v)\bmod p}. The inputs u,v∈[0,p)u,v\in[0,p) are canonical integer representatives of finite field elements. Let s=u+v∈[0,2p−1)⊆[0,2n+1)s=u+v\in[0,2p-1)\subseteq[0,2^{n+1}) be their integer sum. The circuit applies the approximate reduction described above and returns
redκ​(s)=rs+qs​c−qs​ws​2κ=s−qs​p−qs​ws​2κ.\mathrm{red}_{\kappa}(s)=r_{s}+q_{s}c-q_{s}w_{s}2^{\kappa}=s-q_{s}p-q_{s}w_{s}2^{\kappa}.
The circuit can fail to produce the correct result value in two different ways due to the applied approximations. The first is a failure due to the simplified comparison, where the most significant bit serves directly as the comparison bit. If p≤s<2np\leq s<2^{n}, no correction is applied. The result is correct modulo pp but remains larger than pp.
The second failure occurs if the simplified comparison is correct, it causes a correction but the truncated carry propagation modifies the result because the carry propagates further than κ\kappa bits. This happens for qs​ws=1q_{s}w_{s}=1 and the result is 2κ2^{\kappa} too small.
To count failure pairs (u,v)∈[0,p)2(u,v)\in[0,p)^{2}, i.e., pairs that lead to the above two failures, fix an integer value s∈[p,2p−1)s\in[p,2p-1) (no failure occurs for s∈[0,p)s\in[0,p)). For such ss, the number of (u,v)(u,v) with sum s=u+vs=u+v is 2​p−1−s2p-1-s. Summing over all relevant values for ss that lead to a simplified comparison failure yields their overall number as
∑s=p2n−1(2​p−1−s)=c⁡(2​p−c−1)2.\sum_{s=p}^{2^{n}-1}(2p-1-s)=\frac{c(2p-c-1)}{2}.
Failures due to truncated carry propagation occur when qs​ws=1q_{s}w_{s}=1 and rsmod2κ∈[2κ−c,2κ)r_{s}\bmod 2^{\kappa}\in[2^{\kappa}-c,2^{\kappa}). The relevant sums are of the form s=2n+j​2κ−1−bs=2^{n}+j2^{\kappa}-1-b, 1≤j≤2n−κ−11\leq j\leq 2^{n-\kappa}-1, 0≤b<c0\leq b<c. This time, the number of pairs (u,v)∈[0,p)2(u,v)\in[0,p)^{2} satisfying u+v=su+v=s for fixed ss is 2​p−1−s=2n−2​c−j​2κ+b2p-1-s=2^{n}-2c-j2^{\kappa}+b, and again, summing over the relevant values yields their overall count as
∑j=12n−κ−1∑b=0c−1(2n−2​c−j​2κ+b)\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{2^{n-\kappa}-1}\sum_{b=0}^{c-1}\left(2^{n}-2c-j2^{\kappa}+b\right)
=(2n−κ−1)​c​(2n−2​c)−c​2κ​2n−κ2​(2n−κ−1)\displaystyle=(2^{n-\kappa}-1)c(2^{n}-2c)-c2^{\kappa}\frac{2^{n-\kappa}}{2}(2^{n-\kappa}-1)
+(2n−κ−1)​c⁡(c−1)2\displaystyle\qquad+(2^{n-\kappa}-1)\frac{c(c-1)}{2}
=c⁡(2n−κ−1)​2n−3​c−12=c⁡(2n−κ−1)​p−2​c−12.\displaystyle=c(2^{n-\kappa}-1)\frac{2^{n}-3c-1}{2}=c(2^{n-\kappa}-1)\frac{p-2c-1}{2}.
The two cases fall into disjoint events because for the first, the simplified comparison does not lead to a correction (qs=0q_{s}=0), whereas for the second, it does (qs=1q_{s}=1).
A third failure case is a (−1)(-1)-phase that is not corrected due to a failure in the approximate recomputation of the bit qsq_{s} after an XX-measurement. This can happen even if the value of the representative is correctly computed. We are only interested in this case because it is disjoint from the previous two, where all value failures have been accounted for. This means that ws=0w_{s}=0, i.e., redκ​(s)=s−qs​p=rs+qs​c\mathrm{red}_{\kappa}(s)=s-q_{s}p=r_{s}+q_{s}c and the reduction yields the canonical representative.
The bit qsq_{s} can be precisely recovered using the fact that qs=0q_{s}=0 if and only if redκ​(s)≥u\mathrm{red}_{\kappa}(s)\geq u. The comparison used here is approximate, only taking into account the δ\delta most significant bits of redκ​(s)\mathrm{red}_{\kappa}(s) and uu. Therefore, a failed comparison computation can occur if qs=1q_{s}=1 while redκ​(s)=s−p<u\mathrm{red}_{\kappa}(s)=s-p<u, but the top δ\delta bits of both are equal. To count the number of pairs (u,v)∈[0,p)2(u,v)\in[0,p)^{2} for which this failure arises, note that s−p=u+v−p∈[c,p)s-p=u+v-p\in[c,p) and ⌊(s−p)/2n−δ⌋=⌊u/2n−δ⌋\lfloor(s-p)/2^{n-\delta}\rfloor=\lfloor u/2^{n-\delta}\rfloor. We obtain an upper bound by ignoring the condition ws=0w_{s}=0. We count failure pairs (u,v)(u,v) that satisfy c≤u+v−p<u<pc\leq u+v-p<u<p by counting ordered pairs (z=u+v−p,u)(z=u+v-p,u) in [c,p)[c,p) that agree on their most significant n−δn-\delta bits, i.e., pairs that lie in an interval Ij=[j​2n−δ,(j+1)​2n−δ)∩[c,p)I_{j}=[j2^{n-\delta},(j+1)2^{n-\delta})\cap[c,p). Let |Ij|=cj|I_{j}|=c_{j}. The IjI_{j} partition [c,p)[c,p), so ∑jcj=p−c\sum_{j}c_{j}=p-c and each cj≤2n−δc_{j}\leq 2^{n-\delta}. The number of ordered pairs in IjI_{j} is (cj2)\binom{c_{j}}{2} and summing them yields
∑j(cj2)=∑jcj​(cj−1)2≤(2n−δ−1)​p−c2.\sum_{j}\binom{c_{j}}{2}=\sum_{j}\frac{c_{j}(c_{j}-1)}{2}\leq(2^{n-\delta}-1)\frac{p-c}{2}.
The total number of failure pairs covering all three cases is therefore bounded by
Nadd\displaystyle N_{\mathrm{add}}
≤c⁡(2​p−c−1)2+c⁡(2n−κ−1)​p−2​c−12\displaystyle\leq\frac{c(2p-c-1)}{2}+c(2^{n-\kappa}-1)\frac{p-2c-1}{2}
+(2n−δ−1)​p−c2\displaystyle\quad+(2^{n-\delta}-1)\frac{p-c}{2}
=2n−1​c​(1+p−2​c−12κ)+(2n−δ−1)​p−c2.\displaystyle=2^{n-1}c\left(1+\frac{p-2c-1}{2^{\kappa}}\right)+(2^{n-\delta}-1)\frac{p-c}{2}.
For a uniform distribution of (u,v)∈𝔽p×𝔽p(u,v)\in\mathbb{F}_{p}\times\mathbb{F}_{p}, the failure probability due to simplified comparison, truncated carry propagation, or truncated phase correction is
Naddp2<cp+c2κ+1+1+c/p2δ+1.\frac{N_{\mathrm{add}}}{p^{2}}<\frac{c}{p}+\frac{c}{2^{\kappa+1}}+\frac{1+c/p}{2^{\delta+1}}.
Inputs during affine point addition.
Inputs to the approximate addition in our elliptic curve point addition are not uniform random but intermediate results of our elliptic curve point addition.
Let (k,l)(k,l) be a fixed pair of scalars and 0≤i≤2​L−10\leq i\leq 2L-1. Assume no failures in the circuit up to the ii-th point addition step. Let A=(x1,y1)A=(x_{1},y_{1}) be the accumulator point and T=(x2,y2)T=(x_{2},y_{2}) the ii-th lookup point. Approximate addition is used three times in the point addition circuit: to compute (x1−x2)+Rmodp(x_{1}-x_{2})+R\bmod p, 3​x2+(x1−x2+R)modp3x_{2}+(x_{1}-x_{2}+R)\bmod p, and (R+x2−x3)+(−R)modp(R+x_{2}-x_{3})+(-R)\bmod p, where RR is a uniform random mask sampled fresh for each point addition step.
Note that we sample a0∈ℤr∗a_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}_{r}^{*} uniformly at random for each run of the double-scalar multiplication, and μi∈ℤr∖{−j​2i​w:0≤j<2w}\mu_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{r}\setminus\{-j2^{iw}:0\leq j<2^{w}\} and R∈𝔽pR\in\mathbb{F}_{p} are uniform random samples independent of a0a_{0} and each other for the ii-th windowed point addition step. The sample space of triples (a0,μi,R)(a_{0},\mu_{i},R) has size (r−1)​(r−2w)​p(r-1)(r-2^{w})p.
Since we assume no failures up to this point, and with fixed previous random mask values, the map (a0,μi)↦(A,T)(a_{0},\mu_{i})\mapsto(A,T) is injective. We can thus count the number of triples that lead to a given pair of inputs as follows.
For an input (R,x1−x2)(R,x_{1}-x_{2}) to the first addition to be equal to a fixed pair (u,v)∈𝔽p×𝔽p(u,v)\in\mathbb{F}_{p}\times\mathbb{F}_{p}, it has to satisfy R=uR=u and x1−x2=vx_{1}-x_{2}=v, which gives pp possible triples. The two xx-coordinates belong to at most two elliptic curve points each, meaning there are at most 4​p4p pairs (A,T)(A,T) corresponding to the given addition input pair. The same bound holds for the third addition. For the addition with inputs 3​x23x_{2} and x1−x2+Rx_{1}-x_{2}+R, 3​x2=u3x_{2}=u fixes x2x_{2}, yielding at most two curve points. There are at most r−1r-1 possible points AA, each of which then fixes x1x_{1} and thus RR. This means that there are at most 2​(r−1)2(r-1) points per addition input.
Adding these three counts and multiplying their sum by the number of addition failure pairs NaddN_{\mathrm{add}} gives a bound on the number of triples (a0,μi,R)(a_{0},\mu_{i},R) leading to one of the two approximation failures. Dividing by the total number of triples yields an upper bound on the addition failure probability paddp_{\mathrm{add}} during a single elliptic curve point addition as
padd\displaystyle p_{\mathrm{add}}
≤(8​p+2​(r−1))​Naddp⁡(r−1)​(r−2w)≤10​Nadd(r−1)​(r−2w)\displaystyle\leq\frac{(8p+2(r-1))N_{\mathrm{add}}}{p(r-1)(r-2^{w})}\leq\frac{10N_{\mathrm{add}}}{(r-1)(r-2^{w})}
Note that we used r−1<pr-1<p, which is not necessarily true for a general elliptic curve, but holds for 𝚜𝚎𝚌𝚙𝟸𝟻𝟼𝚔𝟷\mathtt{secp256k1}.
Lemma 2.
Let n,w,p,c,r,κ,δn,w,p,c,r,\kappa,\delta be the parameters as described in this section. Let a0a_{0}, μi\mu_{i}, and RR be the random mask values as above. The probability paddp_{\mathrm{add}} over random (a0,μi,R)(a_{0},\mu_{i},R) that either a simplified comparison failure or a carry propagation failure occurs during one of the three approximate additions in the ii-th point addition step is bounded by
padd<10⋅2n−1(r−1)​(r−2w)​(c⁡(1+p−2​c2κ)+p−c2δ).\displaystyle p_{\mathrm{add}}<\frac{10\cdot 2^{n-1}}{(r-1)(r-2^{w})}\left(c(1+\frac{p-2c}{2^{\kappa}})+\frac{p-c}{2^{\delta}}\right).
For the concrete parameters of 𝚜𝚎𝚌𝚙𝟸𝟻𝟼𝚔𝟷\mathtt{secp256k1}, we have n=256n=256 and c=232+977c=2^{32}+977. Our circuits use κ=65\kappa=65, w=16w=16, and δ=32\delta=32 such that
padd<1.7463⋅10−9≈2−29.0931<2−29.093.p_{\mathrm{add}}<1.7463\cdot 10^{-9}\approx 2^{-29.0931}<2^{-29.093}.
Subtraction.
Our modular subtraction circuit is an approximation of |u⟩​|v⟩↦|u⟩​|(v−u)modp⟩\ket{u}\ket{v}\mapsto\ket{u}\ket{(v-u)\bmod p} for u,v∈[0,p)u,v\in[0,p). It is computed using the approximate addition, braced by two modular complements that implement the operation |x⟩↦|p−1−x⟩\ket{x}\mapsto\ket{p-1-x}. More precisely, the circuit realizes
|u⟩​|v⟩\displaystyle\ket{u}\ket{v}
↦|u⟩​|p−1−v⟩\displaystyle\mapsto\ket{u}\ket{p-1-v}
↦|u⟩​|((p−1−v)+u)modp⟩\displaystyle\mapsto\ket{u}\ket{((p-1-v)+u)\bmod p}
↦|u⟩​|p−1−((p−1−v+u)modp)⟩\displaystyle\mapsto\ket{u}\ket{p-1-((p-1-v+u)\bmod p)}
=|u⟩​|(v−u)modp⟩.\displaystyle=\ket{u}\ket{(v-u)\bmod p}.
Since we already obtained a bound on the failure probability due to approximate addition, it remains to bound the failure probability due to the approximate modular complement. The operation |x⟩↦|p−1−x⟩\ket{x}\mapsto\ket{p-1-x} is implemented by a bitwise complement followed by a truncated κ\kappa-bit addition of 2κ−c2^{\kappa}-c. The same result could be computed by a truncated addition of cc to xx followed by a bitwise complement. Without failures, this computes the modular complement because p−1−x=2n−c−1−x=((2n−1)−x)−c=(2n−1)−(x+c)p-1-x=2^{n}-c-1-x=((2^{n}-1)-x)-c=(2^{n}-1)-(x+c), again using the pseudo-Mersenne shape of pp. The only additional source of failures is the truncated carry propagation leading to an incorrect value if xmod2κ≥2κ−cx\bmod 2^{\kappa}\geq 2^{\kappa}-c. Counting failure inputs similarly to the analogous case for the modular addition, we obtain the number of inputs leading to a complement failure as
Ncmpl=c⁡(2n−κ−1),N_{\mathrm{cmpl}}=c(2^{n-\kappa}-1),
which means that for a uniform input, the failure probability due to truncated carry propagation in the modular complement is Ncmpl/p<c/2κN_{\mathrm{cmpl}}/p<c/2^{\kappa}.
Inputs during affine point addition.
Like in the analysis of approximate addition, we fix scalars (k,l)(k,l) and 0≤i≤2​L−10\leq i\leq 2L-1. Approximate subtraction occurs four times, namely to compute x1−x2x_{1}-x_{2}, y1−y2y_{1}-y_{2}, (y3+y2)−y2(y_{3}+y_{2})-y_{2}, and (x2−x3)−x2(x_{2}-x_{3})-x_{2}.
We argue very similarly to the addition case. Given x1,x2x_{1},x_{2}, there are at most 22 elliptic curve points with each xx-coordinate, hence at most 44. For a pair of yy-coordinates, there can be 33 points each due to the corresponding xx-coordinate satisfying a cubic equation, at most 99 points in total. Again, due to bijections mapping the pairs of points before and after the point addition and pairs of values through the ideal subtraction, the same bounds apply to the last two calls as to the first two.
Hence, we can conclude that the probability psubp_{\mathrm{sub}} of modular subtraction approximation failures during a single elliptic-curve point addition is
psub\displaystyle p_{\mathrm{sub}}
≤(4+9+9+4)​Nsub(r−1)​(r−2w).\displaystyle\leq\frac{(4+9+9+4)N_{\mathrm{sub}}}{(r-1)(r-2^{w})}.
Here, Nsub≤Nadd+2​p​NcmplN_{\mathrm{sub}}\leq N_{\mathrm{add}}+2pN_{\mathrm{cmpl}} is the number of input pairs that lead to a failure in the approximate subtraction circuit.
Lemma 3.
Let n,w,p,c,r,κ,δn,w,p,c,r,\kappa,\delta be the parameters as described in this section. Let a0a_{0} and μi\mu_{i} be the random mask values as above. The probability psubp_{\mathrm{sub}} over random (a0,μi)(a_{0},\mu_{i}) that either a borrow propagation failure or a phase failure occurs during one of the four approximate subtractions in the ii-th point addition step is bounded by
psub\displaystyle p_{\mathrm{sub}}
≤135​padd+52​p​c​(2n−κ−1)(r−1)​(r−2w).\displaystyle\leq\frac{13}{5}p_{\mathrm{add}}+\frac{52pc(2^{n-\kappa}-1)}{(r-1)(r-2^{w})}.
Evaluating the expression with the concrete 𝚜𝚎𝚌𝚙𝟸𝟻𝟼𝚔𝟷\mathtt{secp256k1} parameters and implementation parameters κ=65\kappa=65, w=16w=16, and δ=32\delta=32 gives
psub<1.0593796008×10−8≈2−26.4922<2−26.492.p_{\mathrm{sub}}<1.0593796008\times 10^{-8}\approx 2^{-26.4922}<2^{-26.492}.
Negation.
Finally, we analyze an approximation to |u⟩↦|(−u)modp⟩\ket{u}\mapsto\ket{(-u)\bmod p}. Modular negation is computed by a bitwise complement, which gives 2n−1−u2^{n}-1-u and then we subtract c−1c-1 to obtain 2n−c−u=p−u2^{n}-c-u=p-u. The subtraction is approximate by truncating it to κ\kappa bits. This leads to an incorrect value if umod2κ≥2κ−c+1u\bmod 2^{\kappa}\geq 2^{\kappa}-c+1. The integer value p−up-u is the correct canonical representative of the modular negative of uu except if u=0u=0. Therefore, the circuit swaps the values of 00 and pp at the end, leaving all other values intact.
Again, we have two failure cases. The first is due to truncated subtraction of c−1c-1 to κ\kappa bits, happening if umod2κ≥2κ−c+1u\bmod 2^{\kappa}\geq 2^{\kappa}-c+1, and thus is induced by (2n−κ−1)​(c−1)(2^{n-\kappa}-1)(c-1) of the canonical inputs.
The second failure is due to a shortened comparison window for the swap of 00 and pp to the τ\tau most-significant bits of the register. The swap is triggered when all these bits equal the least-significant bit. The circuit assumes that κ≤n−τ\kappa\leq n-\tau and τ≥2\tau\geq 2, otherwise it computes exact negation. Clearly, this comparison implementation has false positives, namely on non-zero even integers that are smaller than 2n−τ2^{n-\tau} and on odd integers in [2n−2n−τ,p)[2^{n}-2^{n-\tau},p). The total number of these failure cases is
(2n−τ−1−1)+2n−τ−c−12=2n−τ−c+32.\left(2^{n-\tau-1}-1\right)+\frac{2^{n-\tau}-c-1}{2}=2^{n-\tau}-\frac{c+3}{2}.
However, we have already counted some of these as failure cases, namely those that have the form u=j​2κ+2κ−c+hu=j2^{\kappa}+2^{\kappa}-c+h, 0≤j<2n−κ−10\leq j<2^{n-\kappa}-1, 1≤h<c1\leq h<c which lead to an intended intermediate value (2n−κ−j−1)​2κ−h(2^{n-\kappa}-j-1)2^{\kappa}-h. Counting the blocks in these intervals, the overlap has size
(2n−τ−κ+1−1)​c−12.(2^{n-\tau-\kappa+1}-1)\frac{c-1}{2}.
Since a failure due to the truncated subtraction cannot be corrected by a short swap failure, the total number of failure inputs for approximate negation is
Nneg\displaystyle N_{\mathrm{neg}}
=(2n−κ−1)​(c−1)+2n−τ−c+32\displaystyle=(2^{n-\kappa}-1)(c-1)+2^{n-\tau}-\frac{c+3}{2}
−(2n−τ−κ+1−1)​c−12\displaystyle\qquad-(2^{n-\tau-\kappa+1}-1)\frac{c-1}{2}
=2n−τ+(c−1)​(2n−κ−2n−τ−κ)−c−1,\displaystyle=2^{n-\tau}+(c-1)(2^{n-\kappa}-2^{n-\tau-\kappa})-c-1,
which means that the failure
probability for uniform input satisfies
Nnegp<c−12κ+1+c/p2τ.\frac{N_{\mathrm{neg}}}{p}<\frac{c-1}{2^{\kappa}}+\frac{1+c/p}{2^{\tau}}.
Inputs during affine point addition.
Modular negation occurs once to compute −x3modp-x_{3}\bmod p. Fixing an input uu therefore fixes x3x_{3}, so there are at most 22 elliptic curve points leading to this xx-coordinate. The table lookup point still can assume any of its r−2wr-2^{w} possible values, leading to 2​(r−2w)2(r-2^{w}) possible curve points leading to input x3x_{3}. With (r−1)​(r−2w)(r-1)(r-2^{w}) possible point pairs, the probability pnegp_{\mathrm{neg}} of a failure due to approximate negation during a single elliptic-curve point addition therefore is bounded by
pneg≤2​Nnegr−1.p_{\mathrm{neg}}\leq\frac{2N_{\mathrm{neg}}}{r-1}.
Lemma 4.
Let n,w,p,c,r,κn,w,p,c,r,\kappa be the parameters as described in this section. Let a0a_{0} and μi\mu_{i} be the random mask values as above. The probability pnegp_{\mathrm{neg}} over random (a0,μi)(a_{0},\mu_{i}) that either a truncated subtraction failure or a shortened comparison failure occurs during the approximate negation in the ii-th point addition step is bounded by
pneg≤2​(2n−τ+(c−1)​(2n−κ−2n−τ−κ)−c−1)r−1.p_{\mathrm{neg}}\leq\frac{2(2^{n-\tau}+(c-1)(2^{n-\kappa}-2^{n-\tau-\kappa})-c-1)}{r-1}.
Setting concrete parameters in our implementation for 𝚜𝚎𝚌𝚙𝟸𝟻𝟼𝚔𝟷\mathtt{secp256k1} with τ=32\tau=32 results in
pneg<6.9850⋅10−10≈2−30.4150<2−30.415.p_{\mathrm{neg}}<6.9850\cdot 10^{-10}\approx 2^{-30.4150}<2^{-30.415}.
B.2.4 Subtracting a square
Consider the subtraction of a square as described in Section VII. It acts on two registers and subtracts the square of the value in the first from that in the second modulo pp. It maps |x⟩​|y⟩\ket{x}\ket{y} to |x⟩​|(y−x2)modp⟩\ket{x}\ket{(y-x^{2})\bmod p}. We use a uniform random mask R∈𝔽pR\in\mathbb{F}_{p} that is added to the target register, which also acts as the accumulator register in the algorithm. The square subtraction circuit then maps
|x⟩​|(y+R)modp⟩↦|x⟩​|(y−x2+R)modp⟩\ket{x}\ket{(y+R)\bmod p}\mapsto\ket{x}\ket{(y-x^{2}+R)\bmod p}
and a subtraction of RR in the second register gives the desired result.
To understand the possible failure cases due to approximate arithmetic, we discuss the specific components as shown in Appendix A. The main operation is detailed for its integer-valued representation in Algorithm 6. It uses the components 𝖠𝖽𝖽𝖡𝖬𝗎𝗅𝗍𝗂𝗉𝗅𝖾±q\mathsf{AddBMultiple}_{\pm}^{q} (Algorithm 9), 𝖠𝖽𝖽𝖢𝖬𝗎𝗅𝗍𝗂𝗉𝗅𝖾−q\mathsf{AddCMultiple}_{-}^{q} (Algorithm 10), 𝖠𝖽𝖽𝖲𝗅𝗂𝖼𝖾σq\mathsf{AddSlice}_{\sigma}^{q} (Algorithm 7), and 𝖯𝗁𝖺𝗌𝖾𝖦𝖤q\mathsf{PhaseGE}^{q} (Algorithm 8). Notation and parameter properties are introduced in Appendix A.
Approximation failures in 𝖠𝖽𝖽𝖲𝗅𝗂𝖼𝖾\mathsf{AddSlice}.
The basic component used in the other algorithms is 𝖠𝖽𝖽𝖲𝗅𝗂𝖼𝖾\mathsf{AddSlice} (Algorithm 7). It operates on two quantum registers, a source register and a target register, holding values ss and vv. It updates the target by adding σ2ts[o,o+w)\sigma 2^{t}s_{[o,o+w)}. The parameter δ\delta provides a way to trade efficiency for accuracy: it determines the number of bits used in the phase comparison, while the parameter ρ∈ℤ≥0\rho\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0} is the carry-padding width for local slice additions. The parameters must satisfy either t+w+ρ<nt+w+\rho<n or t+w=nt+w=n with δ≤w\delta\leq w. There are four possible sources of approximation failures in 𝖠𝖽𝖽𝖲𝗅𝗂𝖼𝖾\mathsf{AddSlice}.
A failure can occur during the complement computation of (p−1)−v(p-1)-v in the case of negative sign σ=−1\sigma=-1. While the bitwise complement operation is exact, the preceding addition of cc may drop a propagating carry. The corresponding failure preimage set is
E~compl={v∈[0,2n)∣v[0,κ)≥2κ−c}\widetilde{E}_{\mathrm{compl}}=\{v\in[0,2^{n})\mid v_{[0,\kappa)}\geq 2^{\kappa}-c\}
and has cardinality c​ 2n−κc\,2^{n-\kappa}.
Complement computation only happens if σ=−1\sigma=-1, but then it occurs twice, once when entering and once when leaving the complemented representation.
Another failure occurs for t+w+ρ<nt+w+\rho<n if in the addition of the source slice s′=s[o,o+w)s^{\prime}=s_{[o,o+w)} into vv, the carry propagates more than w+ρw+\rho bits. The failure preimage set is
E~carry(s,o,t,w)={v∈[0,2n)∣v[t,t+w+ρ)≥2w+ρ−s′}\widetilde{E}_{\mathrm{carry}}(s,o,t,w)=\left\{v\in[0,2^{n})\mid v_{[t,t+w+\rho)}\geq 2^{w+\rho}-s^{\prime}\right\}
with cardinality s′​2n−w−ρ≤(2w−1)​2n−w−ρ<2n−ρs^{\prime}2^{n-w-\rho}\leq(2^{w}-1)2^{n-w-\rho}<2^{n-\rho}.
If t+w=nt+w=n, a carry that propagates more than ww bits means that the sum is at least 2n2^{n}. This carry is captured in an additional qubit, and an approximate reduction modulo pp is applied by adding cc with carry propagation truncated to κ\kappa bits. This failure case is captured by
E~wrap(s,o,t,w)={\displaystyle\widetilde{E}_{\mathrm{wrap}}(s,o,t,w)=\{
v<2n|v+2t​s′≥2n,\displaystyle v<2^{n}\ \mid v+2^{t}s^{\prime}\geq 2^{n},
((v+2ts′)mod2n)[0,κ)≥2κ−c}.\displaystyle((v+2^{t}s^{\prime})\bmod 2^{n})_{[0,\kappa)}\geq 2^{\kappa}-c\}.
Translation by 2ts[o,o+w)2^{t}s_{[o,o+w)} modulo 2n2^{n} is a bijection. Dropping the carry condition therefore upper bounds the cardinality of this set by the usual truncated carry-propagation bound c​2n−κc2^{n-\kappa}.
The fourth failure case in 𝖠𝖽𝖽𝖲𝗅𝗂𝖼𝖾\mathsf{AddSlice} is a failure due to an approximate phase correction in the case t+w=nt+w=n. The extra carry bit can be uncomputed by an XX-measurement and phase correction. Let h=𝟙[v+2ts[o,o+w)≥2n]h=\mathbbm{1}[v+2^{t}s_{[o,o+w)}\geq 2^{n}] and
v+=((v+2ts[o,o+w))mod2n)+hc.v^{+}=((v+2^{t}s_{[o,o+w)})\bmod 2^{n})+hc.
If no failure occurs due to E~wrap​(s,o,t,w)\widetilde{E}_{\mathrm{wrap}}(s,o,t,w), the addition of h​chc has no carry out of the low κ\kappa bits, so v+<2nv^{+}<2^{n}. When m=1m=1 is measured, the exact carry correction contributes the phase (−1)q(-1)^{q}. Together with a full-width call to 𝖯𝗁𝖺𝗌𝖾𝖦𝖤\mathsf{PhaseGE}, it gives
(−1)q(−1)q𝟙[(v+)[t,n)≥s[o,o+w)]=(−1)q𝟙[(v+)[t,n)<s[o,o+w)],(-1)^{q}(-1)^{q\mathbbm{1}[(v^{+})_{[t,n)}\geq s_{[o,o+w)}]}=(-1)^{q\mathbbm{1}[(v^{+})_{[t,n)}<s_{[o,o+w)}]},
which is the required carry-dependent phase. The condition using ≥\geq implemented by 𝖯𝗁𝖺𝗌𝖾𝖦𝖤\mathsf{PhaseGE} is consistent with reconstructing the carry through the condition using <<. The implementation supplies only the most-significant δ\delta bits to 𝖯𝗁𝖺𝗌𝖾𝖦𝖤\mathsf{PhaseGE}, and the resulting reconstruction can fail for two reasons. First, when h=1h=1, the corresponding full-width comparison reconstructs hh exactly if and only if v<pv<p; hence a non-canonical input v≥pv\geq p causes a failure. Second, the comparison on the most-significant δ\delta bits can disagree with the full-width comparison. The failure preimage sets for those events are
E~ph,rep(s,o,t,w)={v∈[0,2n)|v≥p,h=1},\widetilde{E}_{\mathrm{ph,rep}}(s,o,t,w)=\left\{v\in[0,2^{n})\ \middle|\ v\geq p,\quad h=1\right\},
with cardinality at most cc
and (with s′=s[o,o+w)s^{\prime}=s_{[o,o+w)})
E~ph,trunc(s,o,t,w)={\displaystyle\widetilde{E}_{\mathrm{ph,trunc}}(s,o,t,w)=\{
v<2n∣𝟙[(v+)[t,n)≥s′]\displaystyle v<2^{n}\ \mid\mathbbm{1}[(v^{+})_{[t,n)}\geq s^{\prime}]
≠𝟙[(v+)[n−δ,n)≥s[w−δ,w)′]},\displaystyle\neq\mathbbm{1}[(v^{+})_{[n-\delta,n)}\geq s^{\prime}_{[w-\delta,w)}]\},
and |E~ph,trunc​(s,o,t,w)|≤2n−δ|\widetilde{E}_{\mathrm{ph,trunc}}(s,o,t,w)|\leq 2^{n-\delta}.
The bound on the second cardinality follows because if the two comparisons disagree, the δ\delta most significant bits of the values must be equal, fixing δ\delta of the bits of v+v^{+}.
The operation 𝖠𝖽𝖽𝖡𝖬𝗎𝗅𝗍𝗂𝗉𝗅𝖾\mathsf{AddBMultiple} implements an addition or subtraction by a BB-multiple of a register using a BB-adic decomposition. For an integer ss with 0≤s<2ns0\leq s<2^{n_{s}}, where ns∈{n,n+2}n_{s}\in\{n,n+2\}, write s=sL+B​sHs=s_{L}+Bs_{H} with 0≤sL<B0\leq s_{L}<B. Then
B​s=B​sL+B2​sH≡B​sL+c​sH(modp).Bs=Bs_{L}+B^{2}s_{H}\equiv Bs_{L}+cs_{H}\pmod{p}.
The B​sLBs_{L} term is applied by one wrapping call to 𝖠𝖽𝖽𝖲𝗅𝗂𝖼𝖾\mathsf{AddSlice}, and five local calls to compute c​sHcs_{H} using the fixed list TcT_{c}, which is a list containing a signed binary representation of cc. For 𝚜𝚎𝚌𝚙𝟸𝟻𝟼𝚔𝟷\mathtt{secp256k1}, we have Tc=((32,+1),(10,+1),(6,−1),(4,+1),(0,+1))T_{c}=((32,+1),(10,+1),(6,-1),(4,+1),(0,+1)),
such that c=∑(d,ϵ)∈Tcϵ​2d=232+210−26+24+1c=\sum_{(d,\epsilon)\in T_{c}}\epsilon 2^{d}=2^{32}+2^{10}-2^{6}+2^{4}+1.
There are no further target-dependent failure sites outside these six 𝖠𝖽𝖽𝖲𝗅𝗂𝖼𝖾\mathsf{AddSlice} calls.
The operation 𝖠𝖽𝖽𝖢𝖬𝗎𝗅𝗍𝗂𝗉𝗅𝖾\mathsf{AddCMultiple} adds or subtracts a multiple of cc using the signed binary representation list TcT_{c}.
For 0≤s<2n0\leq s<2^{n}, each element in TcT_{c} with d>0d>0 satisfies 2ds=2ds[0,n−d)+2ns[n−d,n)2^{d}s=2^{d}s_{[0,n-d)}+2^{n}s_{[n-d,n)}; if d=0d=0, there is no decomposition. The decomposition into low and high parts at the boundary nn allows the addition of the high parts to be postponed and later corrected by combining them into hs=∑(d,ϵ)∈Tcϵs[n−d,n)h_{s}=\sum_{(d,\epsilon)\in T_{c}}\epsilon s_{[n-d,n)}. This contributes 2n​hs≡c​hs(modp)2^{n}h_{s}\equiv ch_{s}\pmod{p}. For 𝚜𝚎𝚌𝚙𝟸𝟻𝟼𝚔𝟷\mathtt{secp256k1}, where n=256n=256, this is
hs=s[224,256)+s[246,256)−s[250,256)+s[252,256),h_{s}=s_{[224,256)}+s_{[246,256)}-s_{[250,256)}+s_{[252,256)},
and 0≤hs≤c−30\leq h_{s}\leq c-3. Hence 0≤c​hs<2650\leq ch_{s}<2^{65}, so the high correction fits in the fixed 65-bit auxiliary register. The implementation constructs c​hsch_{s} by exact reversible signed additions modulo 2652^{65}. Intermediate values may get reduced modulo 2652^{65}, but the final residue is the ordinary integer c​hsch_{s}. Reversing the same sequence therefore clears the auxiliary qubits.
𝖠𝖽𝖽𝖢𝖬𝗎𝗅𝗍𝗂𝗉𝗅𝖾\mathsf{AddCMultiple} makes five calls to 𝖠𝖽𝖽𝖲𝗅𝗂𝖼𝖾\mathsf{AddSlice} for the direct shifted terms and one call for the high correction c​hsch_{s}. The exact construction and uncomputation of c​hsch_{s} introduce no additional target-dependent failure sites, so all such sites occur in these six calls to 𝖠𝖽𝖽𝖲𝗅𝗂𝖼𝖾\mathsf{AddSlice}.
For 𝚜𝚎𝚌𝚙𝟸𝟻𝟼𝚔𝟷\mathtt{secp256k1}, the square-subtract trace contains three calls to 𝖠𝖽𝖽𝖡𝖬𝗎𝗅𝗍𝗂𝗉𝗅𝖾\mathsf{AddBMultiple}, each containing one call to 𝖠𝖽𝖽𝖲𝗅𝗂𝖼𝖾\mathsf{AddSlice} with three wrapping sites and five 𝖠𝖽𝖽𝖲𝗅𝗂𝖼𝖾\mathsf{AddSlice} calls with three occurrences of the local carry failure sites.
There is one call to the negative version of 𝖠𝖽𝖽𝖢𝖬𝗎𝗅𝗍𝗂𝗉𝗅𝖾\mathsf{AddCMultiple}, which has five 𝖠𝖽𝖽𝖲𝗅𝗂𝖼𝖾\mathsf{AddSlice} calls that can wrap and one local call for the upper bits correction. The negative terms in the representation of cc contribute ten complement failure sites and five phase corrections. Overall, there are 1616 local
𝖠𝖽𝖽𝖲𝗅𝗂𝖼𝖾\mathsf{AddSlice} calls, 99 wrapping calls, and 1313 negative calls, giving 2626
complement sites.
Intermediate results in the algorithm can lie in [0,2n)[0,2^{n}) and thus be non-canonical representations of elements modulo pp. However, under a failure-free execution on canonical inputs, the outputs lie again in [0,p)[0,p) and are thus canonical. This is clear if the control is q=0q=0 as the operation is the identity. For q=1q=1, the last operation of the algorithm that acts on the output register is a modular complement implemented by an addition of cc followed by a bitwise complement in the last line of the 𝖠𝖽𝖽𝖲𝗅𝗂𝖼𝖾\mathsf{AddSlice} algorithm, which is called with negative sign σ=−1\sigma=-1 from 𝖠𝖽𝖽𝖡𝖬𝗎𝗅𝗍𝗂𝗉𝗅𝖾\mathsf{AddBMultiple}. On a failure-free execution, the truncated addition of cc into the vv register right before the complement succeeds, which means that vmod2κ<2κ−cv\bmod 2^{\kappa}<2^{\kappa}-c because no carry has propagated out of the lowest κ\kappa bits. Since v<2nv<2^{n}, we have v+c<2nv+c<2^{n}, thus v<pv<p and the bitwise complement correctly computes (2n−1)−(v+c)=p−1−v∈[0,p)(2^{n}-1)-(v+c)=p-1-v\in[0,p). Therefore, any failure that would lead to a non-canonical output is already accounted for through previous failures.
Overall, under the helper conditions above, and assuming a uniformly
masked target and exact integer squaring operations, residue projection and a union bound
give bounds for the probabilities pvalp_{\mathrm{val}} of complement, wrapping and carry failures and pphasep_{\mathrm{phase}} of phase correction errors as
pval\displaystyle p_{\mathrm{val}}
≤(1+cp)​(162ρ+35​c2κ),\displaystyle\leq\left(1+\frac{c}{p}\right)\left(\frac{16}{2^{\rho}}+\frac{35c}{2^{\kappa}}\right),
pphase\displaystyle p_{\mathrm{phase}}
≤(1+cp)​(92δ+9​c2256).\displaystyle\leq\left(1+\frac{c}{p}\right)\left(\frac{9}{2^{\delta}}+\frac{9c}{2^{256}}\right).
Consequently, the coherent failure probability psqp_{\mathrm{sq}} for the square-subtract operation satisfies
psq≤(1+cp)​(162ρ+35​c2κ+92δ+9​c2256).\displaystyle p_{\mathrm{sq}}\leq\left(1+\frac{c}{p}\right)\left(\frac{16}{2^{\rho}}+\frac{35c}{2^{\kappa}}+\frac{9}{2^{\delta}}+\frac{9c}{2^{256}}\right).
For ρ=δ=32\rho=\delta=32 and κ=65\kappa=65, this gives
psq<2−26.59p_{\mathrm{sq}}<2^{-26.59}.
B.2.5 Approximate in-place multiplication and division
Another component needed in the point addition circuit is an in-place modular multiplication and its inverse, which functions as an in-place modular division, as described in Schrottenloher’s recent work [71]. It is used to compute and uncompute the slope λ\lambda. While we have proved failure bounds for the modular squaring operation and the coordinate offset computations in the previous section, we obtain a bound on the failure probability for the gcd-based in-place multiplication by a Monte Carlo simulation instead. We sampled 1,000,0001{,}000{,}000 uniformly random pairs (u,v)∈𝔽p∗×𝔽p(u,v)\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{*}\times\mathbb{F}_{p}, simulated our approximate circuits to compute |u⟩​|v⟩↦|u⟩​|(u​v)modp⟩\ket{u}\ket{v}\mapsto\ket{u}\ket{(uv)\bmod p} and |u⟩​|v⟩↦|u⟩​|(u−1​v)modp⟩\ket{u}\ket{v}\mapsto\ket{u}\ket{(u^{-1}v)\bmod p}, and counted the number of inputs for which the circuit failed (wrong output, residual phase, deallocation of a nonzero ancilla, etc.). The simulation recorded K=21K=21 failures for both operations.
The circuit parameters we used were mostly identical to the ones used by Schrottenloher [71]. We list the parameters in Table 10.
Table 10: The parameters used in the Monte Carlo simulations of the in-place multiplication circuit. Most of these parameters were described and chosen as in [71].
Parameter
Value
Iteration count
1.413​n+2.4​n1.413n+2.4\sqrt{n}

n−i/1.413+2.3​nn-i/1.413+2.3\sqrt{n}

The simulation allows us to make the following statement using the Clopper-Pearson [18] bound: With confidence level at least 1−2−1291-2^{-129}, the probability that the in-place multiplication procedure using the binary gcd algorithm with the above parameters fails under uniform random inputs (u,v)∈𝔽p∗×𝔽p(u,v)\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{*}\times\mathbb{F}_{p} is bounded by 0.0001493040.000149304. In turn, this allows us to conclude via union bound that with confidence level at least 1−2−1281-2^{-128}, both in-place multiplications succeed with probability at least 1−2⋅0.0001493041-2\cdot 0.000149304 for uniform random inputs from 𝔽p∗×𝔽p\mathbb{F}_{p}^{*}\times\mathbb{F}_{p}.
Since the actual inputs to the in-place multiplication in the circuit are differences of elliptic curve point coordinates, their distribution is not equal to the uniform distribution used in the simulation. This introduces a factor of approximately 44 into the bound inequality, which we will show next.
The actual input to the first in-place modular division algorithm is the pair of differences (x1−x2,y1−y2)(x_{1}-x_{2},y_{1}-y_{2}). Here Ai=(x1,y1)A_{i}=(x_{1},y_{1}) is the current accumulator point after ii point addition steps, i.e., Ai=(x1,y1)=A0+SiA_{i}=(x_{1},y_{1})=A_{0}+S_{i}, where SiS_{i} is the point accumulated into A0A_{0} after ii steps. The point (x2,y2)(x_{2},y_{2}) is the point looked up in the ii-th window from the ii-th lookup table TiT_{i}.
Because a0←$ℤr∗a_{0}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{r}^{*} is sampled uniformly at random and the map A0↦AiA_{0}\mapsto A_{i} as a shift by the point SiS_{i} is injective, and we only need to take into account first-time failures of trajectories that are correct up to this point, the point AiA_{i} can be one of r−1r-1 points, namely in the set E⁡(𝔽p)∖{𝒪}+SiE(\mathbb{F}_{p})\setminus\{\mathcal{O}\}+S_{i}.
The point (x2,y2)(x_{2},y_{2}) is of the form [j​2i​w+μi]​P[j2^{iw}+\mu_{i}]P or [j​2(i−L)​w+μi]​Q[j2^{(i-L)w}+\mu_{i}]Q. Since μi\mu_{i} is sampled uniformly at random from ℤr∖{−2i​w​j∣j∈{0,1,…,2w−1}}\mathbb{Z}_{r}\setminus\{-2^{iw}j\mid j\in\{0,1,\dots,2^{w}-1\}\} or ℤr∖{−2(i−L)​w​j∣j∈{0,1,…,2w−1}}\mathbb{Z}_{r}\setminus\{-2^{(i-L)w}j\mid j\in\{0,1,\dots,2^{w}-1\}\}, the point can be any point in E⁡(𝔽p)E(\mathbb{F}_{p}) except for the set of points of size 2w2^{w} given by the exceptions above.
The set of possible input pairs ((x1,y1),(x2,y2))((x_{1},y_{1}),(x_{2},y_{2})) therefore has size (r−1)​(r−2w)(r-1)(r-2^{w}), and we need to
relate the distribution of input pairs (x1−x2,y1−y2)(x_{1}-x_{2},y_{1}-y_{2}) to the uniform random distribution of pairs (u,v)∈𝔽p∗×𝔽p(u,v)\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{*}\times\mathbb{F}_{p} that was used in our Monte Carlo simulation.
The u=0u=0 case is already covered by our exceptional point analysis, so we may assume u≠0u\neq 0.
Fix a specific pair (u,v)(u,v) with u≠0u\neq 0.
A given difference of curve point coordinates is equal to (u,v)(u,v) if and only if x1=x2+ux_{1}=x_{2}+u and y1=y2+vy_{1}=y_{2}+v.
Using that both points satisfy the curve equation, we obtain
y22+2​y2​v+v2\displaystyle y_{2}^{2}+2y_{2}v+v^{2}
=y12=x13+b\displaystyle=y_{1}^{2}=x_{1}^{3}+b
=x23+3​x22​u+3​x2​u2+u3+b,\displaystyle=x_{2}^{3}+3x_{2}^{2}u+3x_{2}u^{2}+u^{3}+b,
which, together with y22=x23+by_{2}^{2}=x_{2}^{3}+b, gives
2​v​y2+v2=3​x22​u+3​x2​u2+u3.2vy_{2}+v^{2}=3x_{2}^{2}u+3x_{2}u^{2}+u^{3}.
If v≠0v\neq 0, y2y_{2} can be expressed by a term quadratic in x2x_{2}, and substituting this expression into the curve equation shows that x2x_{2} satisfies a polynomial equation of degree 44. Hence, there are at most 44 possible values for x2x_{2}, yielding at most 44 solutions for pairs (x2,y2)(x_{2},y_{2}).
If v=0v=0, then y1=y2y_{1}=y_{2} and x2x_{2} satisfies a quadratic polynomial equation, yielding at most 22 solutions for x2x_{2} and again at most 44 pairs in total.
The same bound of at most 44 possible curve point pairs yielding a given pair of differences applies to the second call of the binary-gcd-based circuit, the in-place modular multiplication (u,v)→(u,u​v(modp))(u,v)\rightarrow(u,uv\pmod{p}). After an execution of an ideal in-place multiplication, the outputs are x2−x3x_{2}-x_{3} and y2+y3=y2−(−y3)y_{2}+y_{3}=y_{2}-(-y_{3}). These are again coordinate differences, where (x3,y3)=(x1,y1)+(x2,y2)(x_{3},y_{3})=(x_{1},y_{1})+(x_{2},y_{2}) are the coordinates of the (negative of the) accumulator point Ai+1A_{i+1} for the next iteration and the lookup point PiP_{i}, and so the factor-of-four bound holds at the point in the circuit.
Now, the mapping (Ai,Pi)(A_{i},P_{i}) to (Pi,−Ai+1)(P_{i},-A_{i+1}) is a bijection, as is the ideal, non-approximate in-place multiplication for non-zero uu (u=0u=0 corresponds to an exceptional case already accounted for). As a result, there is a one-to-one correspondence between the multiplier inputs that fail and the corresponding (ideal) outputs, each of which corresponds to at most four curve point pairs.
Let qdivq_{\mathrm{div}} and qmulq_{\mathrm{mul}} be the failure probabilities of the in-place division and multiplication circuits for pairs (u,v)(u,v) sampled uniformly at random. Hence there are at most p⁡(p−1)​qdivp(p-1)q_{\mathrm{div}} and p⁡(p−1)​qmulp(p-1)q_{\mathrm{mul}} failure pairs in 𝔽p∗×𝔽p\mathbb{F}_{p}^{*}\times\mathbb{F}_{p}. Each can be reached by at most 44 pairs of points under the difference map. The probability pg​c​d,divp_{gcd,\mathrm{div}} over uniform random samples of a0a_{0} and μi\mu_{i} that an input point pair leads to a failure in the division algorithm is at most
pg​c​d,div≤4​p​(p−1)(r−1)​(r−2w)​qdiv,p_{gcd,\mathrm{div}}\leq\frac{4p(p-1)}{(r-1)(r-2^{w})}q_{\mathrm{div}},
and analogously for the in-place multiplication
pg​c​d,mul≤4​p​(p−1)(r−1)​(r−2w)​qmul.p_{gcd,\mathrm{mul}}\leq\frac{4p(p-1)}{(r-1)(r-2^{w})}q_{\mathrm{mul}}.
Overall, the probability that a failure occurs in one of the in-place binary gcd circuits over the random uniform choices of a0a_{0} and μi\mu_{i} is bounded by ≈4​(qdiv+qmul)\approx 4(q_{\mathrm{div}}+q_{\mathrm{mul}}):
pg​c​d,div+pg​c​d,mul≤4​p​(p−1)(r−1)​(r−2w)​(qdiv+qmul).p_{gcd,\mathrm{div}}+p_{gcd,\mathrm{mul}}\leq\frac{4p(p-1)}{(r-1)(r-2^{w})}(q_{\mathrm{div}}+q_{\mathrm{mul}}).
B.3 Failure probability bound
Using the above failure probabilities for all the components in the computation of f~ω​(k,l)\widetilde{f}_{\omega}(k,l), we obtain the overall failure bound pfp_{f} as follows.
We start by bounding the failure probability pecaddp_{\mathrm{ecadd}} for a single affine elliptic curve point addition using a union bound over all possible failure types for the separate components. We thus find that with probability 1−2−1281-2^{-128},
pecadd\displaystyle p_{\mathrm{ecadd}}
≤psub+pg​c​d,div+padd+psq+pg​c​d,mul+pneg\displaystyle\leq p_{\mathrm{sub}}+p_{gcd,\mathrm{div}}+p_{\mathrm{add}}+p_{\mathrm{sq}}+p_{gcd,\mathrm{mul}}+p_{\mathrm{neg}}
≤0.001195.\displaystyle\leq 0.001195.
Then, via union bound on the 2828 point additions, we have that
pf≤28​pecadd+6​Lr−1≤0.03346.p_{f}\leq 28p_{\mathrm{ecadd}}+\frac{6L}{r-1}\leq 0.03346.
B.4 Algorithm success probability with approximate arithmetic
In this section, we derive a bound on the success probability of Shor’s algorithm for solving the ECDLP when using approximate double scalar multiplication with failure bound pfp_{f}. Our bound takes into account wrong outputs as well as phase errors due to approximate phase-fix predicates.
Recall the failure set
Bω={(k,l)∈\displaystyle B_{\omega}=\{(k,l)\in
[0,2n)×[0,2n)∣\displaystyle[0,2^{n})\times[0,2^{n})\mid
fω(k,l)≠f~ω(k,l)∨sω(k,l)=−1}\displaystyle f_{\omega}(k,l)\neq\widetilde{f}_{\omega}(k,l)\vee s_{\omega}(k,l)=-1\}
for the masked approximate implementation f~ω​(k,l)\tilde{f}_{\omega}(k,l), i.e., the set of inputs (k,l)(k,l) for which the masked approximate implementation does not agree with the ideal masked value fω​(k,l)f_{\omega}(k,l) or it agrees but the implementation produces a residual (−1)(-1)-phase.
For the analysis, we assume that we perform the known translation from fω​(k,l)f_{\omega}(k,l) to f⁡(k,l)f(k,l) right after applying the oracle, i.e., we assume that the random point offsets of the masked implementation are removed. Because the projector onto successful outcomes of the algorithm (see the definition of MM below) only acts on the input register, this shift does not affect outcome probabilities, and thus does not have to be implemented in practice. However, this assumption simplifies the analysis significantly.
For brevity, we will write x∈X:=[0,2n)×[0,2n)x\in X:=[0,2^{n})\times[0,2^{n}) instead of (k,l)(k,l) and f⁡(x)f(x) instead of f⁡(k,l)f(k,l).
Let N=22​n=|X|N=2^{2n}=|X| and the ideal state before the QFT-measurement
|Ψ⟩=1N​∑x∈X|x⟩​|f⁡(x)⟩.\ket{\Psi}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{x\in X}\ket{x}\ket{f(x)}.
The corresponding approximate state is
|Ψ~ω⟩=1N​∑x∈Xsω​(x)​|x⟩​|f~ω​(x)⟩,\ket{\tilde{\Psi}_{\omega}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{x\in X}s_{\omega}(x)\ket{x}\ket{\tilde{f}_{\omega}(x)},
where sω​(x)∈{−1,1}s_{\omega}(x)\in\{-1,1\} tracks the phase error for label xx. We thus have sω​(x)=1s_{\omega}(x)=1 and f~ω​(x)=f​(x)\tilde{f}_{\omega}(x)=f(x) for all x∉Bωx\notin B_{\omega}.
Let the projector onto the successful outcome set SS after the inverse QFT be ∑y∈S|y⟩​⟨y|\sum_{y\in S}\ket{y}\bra{y}. Before the inverse QFT, this corresponds to the projector
M=(QFT​∑y∈S|y⟩​⟨y|​QFT†)⊗𝟙,M=\left(\text{QFT}\sum_{y\in S}\ket{y}\bra{y}\text{QFT}^{\dagger}\right)\otimes\mathbbm{1},
and we have that the success probabilities
P=⟨Ψ|M|Ψ⟩​ and ​P~=𝔼ω​[⟨Ψ~ω|M|Ψ~ω⟩].P=\braket{\Psi|M|\Psi}\;\text{ and }\;\tilde{P}=\mathbb{E}_{\omega}[\braket{\tilde{\Psi}_{\omega}|M|\tilde{\Psi}_{\omega}}].
We now write the difference between the ideal and the expected state 𝔼ω​[|Ψ~ω⟩]\mathbb{E}_{\omega}[\ket{\tilde{\Psi}_{\omega}}] as
|Δ⟩:=𝔼ω​[|Ψ~ω⟩]−|Ψ⟩=1N​∑x∈X|x⟩​|dx⟩,\ket{\Delta}:=\mathbb{E}_{\omega}[\ket{\tilde{\Psi}_{\omega}}]-\ket{\Psi}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{x\in X}\ket{x}\ket{d_{x}},
with |dx⟩:=𝔼ω​[1x∈Bω​(sω​(x)​|f~ω​(x)⟩−|f⁡(x)⟩)]\ket{d_{x}}:=\mathbb{E}_{\omega}[1_{x\in B_{\omega}}(s_{\omega}(x)\ket{\tilde{f}_{\omega}(x)}-\ket{f(x)})].
Recall that pfp_{f} is such that for all x∈Xx\in X,
Prω[x∈Bω]≤pf.\Pr_{\omega}[x\in B_{\omega}]\leq p_{f}.
Therefore, ‖|dx⟩‖≤2​pf\|\ket{d_{x}}\|\leq 2p_{f} from the triangle inequality for expectations and
‖𝔼ω​[|Ψ~ω⟩]−|Ψ⟩‖2=1N​∑x∈X‖|dx⟩‖2≤4N​∑x∈Xpf2=4​pf2.\|\mathbb{E}_{\omega}[\ket{\tilde{\Psi}_{\omega}}]-\ket{\Psi}\|^{2}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{x\in X}\|\ket{d_{x}}\|^{2}\leq\frac{4}{N}\sum_{x\in X}p_{f}^{2}=4p_{f}^{2}.
Using Jensen’s inequality and the fact that MM is a projector independent of ω\omega, we have
P~\displaystyle\tilde{P}
=𝔼ω​[‖M​|Ψ~ω⟩‖2]≥‖M​𝔼ω​[|Ψ~ω⟩]‖2\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\omega}[\|M\ket{\tilde{\Psi}_{\omega}}\|^{2}]\geq\left\|M\mathbb{E}_{\omega}[\ket{\tilde{\Psi}_{\omega}}]\right\|^{2}
≥(max⁡{0,‖M​|Ψ⟩‖−‖M​|Δ⟩‖})2\displaystyle\geq\left(\max\{0,\|M\ket{\Psi}\|-\|M\ket{\Delta}\|\}\right)^{2}
≥(max⁡{0,P−2​pf})2.\displaystyle\geq\left(\max\{0,\sqrt{P}-2p_{f}\}\right)^{2}.
Given a lower bound P0P_{0} with P0≤PP_{0}\leq P, we thus have
P~≥(max⁡{0,P0−2​pf})2.\tilde{P}\geq\left(\max\{0,\sqrt{P_{0}}-2p_{f}\}\right)^{2}.

Appendix D Circuit synthesis
Circuit synthesis turns the verified hierarchy of specs and defs into an
architecture-legal, executable schedule. The entry point takes a root spec and
its parameters, a target architecture, an optional layout plan, and synthesis
options; it returns the measurement schedule together with its metrics and a
serialized schedule artifact from which all downstream reporting is derived.
The architecture object declares a group of memory blocks and one or more
groups of factory blocks. The memory type fixes block size and shape, which
operations are directly legal within one block or between two blocks, and how
many concurrent operations and logical measurements a block supports. Each
factory group names a resource-state kind, a block count, the number of
states per block, and the preparation and cleanup recipes for that factory
type—for the ECC pipeline, CCZ\mathrm{CCZ} factories. Legality checks, placement, and
resource allocation all key off this object.
D.1 Synthesis tree and frontier lowering
Synthesis expands the root into a synthesis tree that records the decomposing
hierarchy, the def selected at each node, qubit and classical-bit bindings, and
classical conditions on components. The frontier lowering loop repeatedly selects a frontier
node, chooses a def if the node is not directly legal on the architecture,
allocates and binds the def’s ancillae, and expands the node—until every leaf
is architecture-legal.
Routing is handled by placement and by local
measurement-based gadgets rather than a separate global SWAP\mathrm{SWAP} network: an
operation that is not legal in the current geometry is decomposed into
components such as logical measurements, controlled loads, CNOT\mathrm{CNOT} fanout, or
PcP gadgets (though we managed to entirely avoid naively decomposing PcPs in
this work).
Schedule-dependent resource binding is deferred during this refinement:
factory ancillae are virtual qubits in the tree,
so defs and legality checks remain coherent while the
physical factory assignment is chosen later, at scheduling time.
Run-local facts—qubit ownership, ordering dependencies—are
recorded in synthesis-side state and never leak into the reusable
specs and defs. Two further mechanisms keep synthesis tractable at
application scale: a compact representation of repeated structure, and
memoized replay of previously lowered subtrees.
D.2 Compact repetition and deferred parameters
Repeated algorithmic structure is kept as a compact repeated subcircuit rather
than expanded into a separate copy for every iteration. Its compile-time bounds
determine how many times the body executes, while an iteration variable may
select the operand qubit, register slice, constant, or implementation variant
used on each pass. Repetitions may be nested or traversed in reverse, and
inverting a repeated computation reverses both the iteration order and the
enclosed operations. This representation is used for repeated shifts,
multiplier digit sweeps, lookup traversal, modular-inversion rounds, and the
repeated work units above point addition.
The repeated body remains compact while the compiler selects implementations,
checks architectural legality, estimates costs, and records the synthesis
state. When different iterations genuinely require different circuit shapes,
the alternatives are selected by the iteration variable; changing register
widths are a common example.
Only when the final operation stream is emitted
does the compiler substitute concrete operands and iteration-local classical
results for every pass. This late expansion reduces compilation work and memory
even though the executable schedule ultimately contains every operation.
D.3 Memoized lowering and subtree replay
The second mechanism is a dynamic-programming-style replay cache.
After a subtree has been lowered completely, the compiler records its expansion
under a structural key containing the source spec type and normalized
parameters, the chosen def, and compatible ancilla bindings. These immutable
tuples are hashable, so a repeated semantic component can locate an earlier
lowering with a dictionary lookup rather than repeating recursive def
selection, child-support tests, allotment, and layout decisions. Separate
run-local caches remember preferred defs and architecture-support results.
On a replay hit, the compiler reproduces the cached child structure under the
new occurrence and remaps occurrence-local classical identities. The replayed
state includes the selected decomposition, ancilla bindings, sibling topology,
factory-loan annotations, and qubit-ownership classes; it is not merely a copy
of a flattened gate string. A candidate is rejected when its completed subtree
or resource context is not safe to reuse, in which case ordinary expansion is
performed.
Rewinding or editing the live tree invalidates the affected cached
state. These restrictions make hashing an optimization only: enabling or
disabling replay must not change the emitted program.
This reuse happens during hierarchical synthesis, before measurement
scheduling: the scheduler receives the bound serial stream and constructs
concrete layers for every occurrence, so the replay speedup is a lowering
speedup. Scheduler-side scalability comes instead from the packed layer
representation and the optional late resolution of classical conditions
described next.
D.4 Measurement scheduling
Beyond the general routine described in the main text, the scheduler applies a
resource-pool model to state injections.
Each CCZ\mathrm{CCZ} factory use is split into an injection
(three Z​ZZZ logical measurements, each coupling one CCZ\mathrm{CCZ} state qubit to its memory target)
and factory cleanup measurements. The
scheduler tracks the number of available resource states per factory kind,
decrementing on injection and incrementing on cleanup, and when a cleanup is
delayed the propagating phase fix is tracked as a conditional Pauli byproduct.
Only after scheduling are virtual factory indices assigned to physical factory
blocks, pairing each injection with its matching cleanup. Internally, layers
are stored as block-local Pauli bitmasks and flat instruction buffers rather
than per-gate objects—the representation change that makes scheduling
application-scale circuits tractable.
D.5 Layout plans
A layout plan is an authored, scoped placement policy for one spec
implementation. It can force the def choice for its spec (and, through nested
child plans, for the children) and binds semantic ports and ancillae to
placement rules: serial strips through memory blocks, interleaved cycles, or
direct block-and-slot coordinates. It can also reserve pools of helper qubits
for Pauli-controlled-Pauli (PcP) gadgets. The plan is where
design-level decisions enter: which registers are colocated, which resource
pools exist, and which defs are forced. Below the planned shape the compiler
still applies its local policies, but changing the plan changes the compiled
tree, the schedule pressure, and the resulting metrics.

Algorithm-wide exhaustion budget.
For a reservoir of capacity rr, we call the period between consecutive
reservoir refills a refill interval, and let XX be the number of
replacement ions demanded during that interval. We define exhaustion
as the event X>rX>r, in which the replacement demand exceeds the available
stock before the next refill. We assume that exhaustion of any local or global
reservoir counts as a failed run and choose the reservoir capacities to bound
the total run-wide exhaustion probability below the target
εres=10−3.\varepsilon_{\mathrm{res}}=10^{-3}.
(64)
Meeting this target ensures that reservoir exhaustion contributes at most
0.10.1 percentage points to the failure probability of an attempt.

operates for at most Dmeas=75,251,329D_{\mathrm{meas}}=75{,}251{,}329 measurement layers, as listed in

Cat-state bundle pairs and Bell-state resources.
Let kcatk_{\mathrm{cat}} denote the maximum number of logical measurements that
the device must support in parallel. The reuse schedule of
Sec. XII.3.2 assigns two mobile cat-state bundles to each
measurement stream. For Q102, each bundle contains one weight-3030 cat-state
register and one weight-3030 verification bank; the weight 3030 is set by
the widest logical measurement made in a Q102 block. Together, the two bundles
allocated to one measurement stream form a cat-state bundle pair. Each
bundle occupies 2×30=602\times 30=60 physical qubits, so each bundle pair occupies
120120 physical qubits. The resulting footprint is therefore
nphyscat​(kcat)=2​kcat​(2×30)=120​kcat.n_{\mathrm{phys}}^{\mathrm{cat}}(k_{\mathrm{cat}})=2k_{\mathrm{cat}}(2\times 30)=120k_{\mathrm{cat}}.
(79)
The peak compiled-circuit requirement is
kcat(peak)=24k_{\mathrm{cat}}^{(\mathrm{peak})}=24 simultaneous logical measurements, as
listed in Table 11. The cat-state allocation
is therefore
nphyscat=120​kcat(peak)=120​(24)=2,880.n_{\mathrm{phys}}^{\mathrm{cat}}=120k_{\mathrm{cat}}^{(\mathrm{peak})}=120(24)=2{,}880.
(80)
In each cat-state production round, every LM2 measurement involving
memory blocks consumes
kBellk_{\mathrm{Bell}} Bell pairs to stitch the two cat states used in that
round, where kBellk_{\mathrm{Bell}} is the number of pairs required to verify the
stitched cat state with undetected-error probability at most ε\varepsilon.
Following the Walking Cat stitching
protocol [78], each Bell pair is prepared
with a small unitary circuit.
One qubit from each pair is routed to each of the two target cat-state
factories. At the two factories,
we measure a joint Z​ZZZ-stabilizer parity check between each Bell-pair qubit and one qubit of the local cat state.
This procedure stitches the two cat states into a larger,
joint cat state. Each Bell pair supplies one stabilizer measurement of the joint cat state, so the
kBellk_{\mathrm{Bell}} pairs provide kBellk_{\mathrm{Bell}} redundant stitch
checks. Writing p′≤4​pp^{\prime}\leq 4p for the error rate of one stitch check,
including Bell-pair and transport faults, an incorrect parity can induce
a high-weight XX error on one half of the stitched cat state, but the stitch
is rejected unless all kBellk_{\mathrm{Bell}} stitch checks return the same wrong
value.
At p=10−4p=10^{-4}, choosing kBell=4k_{\mathrm{Bell}}=4 gives
(p′)kBell≤(4​p)4=2.56×10−14(p^{\prime})^{k_{\mathrm{Bell}}}\leq(4p)^{4}=2.56\times 10^{-14}, below the target
precision ε=10−11\varepsilon=10^{-11}. We provision one Bell-state
bundle for each of the 12 simultaneous LM2 measurements at peak demand. Each
bundle therefore contains 2​kBell=82k_{\mathrm{Bell}}=8 physical qubits, including
while those qubits are in flight. The four independent pairs can be prepared in
parallel in two POCs, well within the 13.45–14.8-POC cat-state production
interval; we assume that pre-positioning the bundles similarly hides their
transport latency, so no additional Bell pairs are required for pipelining.
Therefore, our bell-state allocation is:
nphysBell,LM2=12×kBell×2=12×4×2=96.n_{\mathrm{phys}}^{\mathrm{Bell,LM2}}=12\times k_{\mathrm{Bell}}\times 2=12\times 4\times 2=96.
(81)
Magic factories.
The throughput analysis in Sec. XII.5 specifies a total of four
Eastinthillation factories. Each factory contains one Q66 block, two dedicated
C6 blocks, a local reservoir provisioned with the
three Q66 replacement ions and two replacement ions for each C6 block from
Table 13, and three weight-16 cat-state factories.
Including syndrome ancillas, each Q66 block
and its two C6 blocks occupy 2​(66)2(66) and
2×2​(6)2\times 2(6) physical qubits, respectively, while each
cat-state factory contains two weight-16 registers and one bank of 16
verification ancillas, occupying 3×163\times 16 physical qubits. Finally, we
count one additional six-qubit C6 Bell-state source for each C6 block, as
required by the measurement construction in Sec. XII.5. Thus, the
footprint of one magic factory is
nphysmagic\displaystyle n_{\mathrm{phys}}^{\mathrm{magic}}
=2​(66)+2×2​(6)+(3+2×2)\displaystyle=2(66)+2\times 2(6)+(3+2\times 2)
(82)
+3​(3×16)+2​(6)\displaystyle}{\displaystyle+3(3\times 16)+2(6)
=319,\displaystyle=319,
and the four factories occupy
Nphysmagic=4​nphysmagic=4×319=1,276N_{\mathrm{phys}}^{\mathrm{magic}}=4n_{\mathrm{phys}}^{\mathrm{magic}}=4\times 319=1{,}276
(83)
physical qubits in total.
Combining the peak allocation of each component type with the global reservoir
gives the device footprint
Nphys\displaystyle N_{\mathrm{phys}}
=69​(207)+24​(120)+12​(8)\displaystyle=69(207)+24(120)+12(8)
+4​(207)+4​(319)+34=19,397.\displaystyle\quad{}+4(207)+4(319)+34=19{,}397.
(84)
E.2 Runtime
The compiled algorithm has measurement-layer depth
Dmeas=75,251,329D_{\mathrm{meas}}=75{,}251{,}329, as listed in
Table 11. To obtain an upper bound on the
runtime, we assign every measurement layer the mean duration of a CCZ\mathrm{CCZ}
injection. Among the logical measurements used in the compiled elliptic-curve
point-addition circuits, a CCZ\mathrm{CCZ} injection has the longest mean duration. It
also determines the critical path whenever it appears in a parallel layer,
because its outcome is needed to resolve subsequent Clifford corrections on
the data blocks. The three independent Viterbi processes in a CCZ\mathrm{CCZ} injection
have a joint average stopping time of 5.465.46 Q102 SECs, as derived in
eq. 26. Since one Q102 SEC takes 27 POCs, this
gives Tinj=147.48T_{\mathrm{inj}}=147.48 POCs, or approximately 0.029500.02950 seconds, when
evaluated using the unrounded duration. The
resulting expected-runtime upper bound per attempt is therefore
Trun\displaystyle T_{\mathrm{run}}
≲Dmeas×0.0294958​s\displaystyle\lesssim D_{\mathrm{meas}}\times 0.0294958~\mathrm{s}
=75,251,329×0.0294958​s\displaystyle=75{,}251{,}329\times 0.0294958~\mathrm{s}
=2,219,600​s≈25.7​days.\displaystyle=2{,}219{,}600~\mathrm{s}\approx 25.7~\mathrm{days}.
(85)
E.3 Logical failure probability
We include five contributions to the algorithm-wide logical failure
probability: memory errors in the Q102 blocks, errors in logical measurements,
errors in the CCZ\mathrm{CCZ} states consumed by Toffoli gates, errors in the TT states
consumed by the iQFT, and reservoir exhaustion.
Memory errors.
As an upper bound, we assume that all
Dmeas=75,251,329D_{\mathrm{meas}}=75{,}251{,}329 measurement
layers occur on all 6969 Q102 memory blocks in the device. But, in actuality,
only a small subset of qubits needs to
hold quantum information continuously for this whole time in the application, so
our upper bound is quite conservative. We find
NSEC\displaystyle N_{\mathrm{SEC}}
=Dmeas×τCCZavg×69\displaystyle=D_{\mathrm{meas}}\times\tau_{\mathrm{CCZ}}^{\mathrm{avg}}\times 69
=75,251,329×τCCZavg×69\displaystyle=75{,}251{,}329\times\tau_{\mathrm{CCZ}}^{\mathrm{avg}}\times 69
=2.83615×1010.\displaystyle=2.83615\times 10^{10}.
(86)
At the Q102 logical error rate
pL,SECQ102=9.34×10−12p_{\mathrm{L,SEC}}^{\mathrm{Q102}}=9.34\times 10^{-12} per SEC from
Table 4, this corresponds to a memory-failure
probability of
pmem=1−(1−pL,SECQ102)NSEC=0.2327.p_{\mathrm{mem}}=1-\left(1-p_{\mathrm{L,SEC}}^{\mathrm{Q102}}\right)^{N_{\mathrm{SEC}}}=0.2327.
(87)
The four Q102 logical CliNR blocks hold encoded states only for the duration
of frame clearing, rather than for the full algorithm. Their exposure is
therefore included in the non-Toffoli logical-measurement count below, not in
the long-lived memory-failure total.
Although the calculated memory-failure probability is relatively high, our
estimate is substantially inflated by the assumption that all 69 Q102 blocks
continuously hold quantum information throughout the algorithm. The
probability can also be readily reduced by increasing the code distance or
tuning the decoder, as discussed for the memory-error bottleneck in the magic
factory.
Logical measurement errors.
For a weight-3030 Viterbi measurement at physical error rate p=10−4p=10^{-4},
the single-round bit-flip model gives
pflip,30=2.1×30×10−4=6.3×10−3p_{\mathrm{flip},30}=2.1\times 30\times 10^{-4}=6.3\times 10^{-3}.
A vote margin of five therefore has logical error probability
pVit,30=[1+(1−pflip,30pflip,30)5]−1=1.02×10−11.p_{\mathrm{Vit},30}=\left[1+\left(\frac{1-p_{\mathrm{flip},30}}{p_{\mathrm{flip},30}}\right)^{5}\right]^{-1}=1.02\times 10^{-11}.
(88)
For Nnon​-​Tof=369,882,166N_{\mathrm{non\text{-}Tof}}=369{,}882{,}166 such non-Toffoli logical measurements,
as listed in Table 11, their combined
failure probability is
1−(1−pVit,30)Nnon​-​Tof=0.003781-(1-p_{\mathrm{Vit},30})^{N_{\mathrm{non\text{-}Tof}}}=0.00378.
Eastinthillation factory errors.
The Eastinthillation factory has logical error rate
pLCCZ≲9.36×10−10p_{\mathrm{L}}^{\mathrm{CCZ}}\lesssim 9.36\times 10^{-10} per accepted state
from eq. 60. Using one accepted state for
each of NTof=40,420,330N_{\mathrm{Tof}}=40{,}420{,}330 Toffoli
injections from Table 11, the cumulative
failure probability of the Eastinthillation factories is
pCCZ=1−(1−pLCCZ)NTof≲0.0371.p_{\mathrm{CCZ}}=1-\left(1-p_{\mathrm{L}}^{\mathrm{CCZ}}\right)^{N_{\mathrm{Tof}}}\lesssim 0.0371.
(89)
iQFT-stage errors.
The 5,6845{,}684 accepted TT-state pairs and 464464 synthesized rotations
used for the iQFT contribute
piQFT≤7.64×10−5,p_{\mathrm{iQFT}}\leq 7.64\times 10^{-5},
(90)
as calculated in eq. 61.
Reservoir exhaustion.
The device’s reservoir-exhaustion
probability is at most 7.32×10−47.32\times 10^{-4}.
Assuming these five failure mechanisms are statistically independent, their
contributions combine to give
pfail,alg\displaystyle p_{\mathrm{fail,alg}}
≤1−(1−0.2327)​(1−0.00378)\displaystyle\leq 1-(1-0.2327)(1-0.00378)
×(1−0.0371)​(1−7.64×10−5)\displaystyle\quad{}\times(1-0.0371)(1-7.64\times 10^{-5})
×(1−7.32×10−4)\displaystyle\quad{}\times(1-7.32\times 10^{-4})
=0.2646≈26%.\displaystyle=0.2646\approx 26\%.
(91)
Appendix F Swap-loss sensitivity
We choose pswap=0.5p_{\mathrm{swap}}=0.5 as a conservative order-one swap
probability. The precise choice has little effect on the reported logical error
rate: with the swap-resilient LDU, the simulations show no discernible dependence
on pswapp_{\mathrm{swap}} over the tested range [0,0.75][0,0.75], within statistical
uncertainty, as shown in Fig. 23.
Figure 23: Swap-loss sensitivity of the [[102,22,9]][[102,22,9]] code. The logical
error rate per SEC is shown as a function of pswapp_{\mathrm{swap}} at
p=10−3p=10^{-3}, pleak=10−4p_{\mathrm{leak}}=10^{-4}, and
ploss=10−6p_{\mathrm{loss}}=10^{-6}. Each point uses 10610^{6} shots of nine
SECs, and the error bars are exact 95% binomial confidence intervals
converted to per-SEC rates. The red ring marks the default simulation setting
pswap=0.5p_{\mathrm{swap}}=0.5.

support. To report errors in the HTML that will help us improve conversion and rendering, choose any of the
methods listed below:

Tip: You can select the relevant text first, to include it in your report.

may not have found yet. Your efforts will help us improve the HTML versions for all readers, because disability
should not be a barrier to accessing research. Thank you for your continued support in championing open access for
all.
